Rows and Capabilities as Modal Effects Journal: PACMPL Volume: 1 CONF 1
Wenhao Tang, Sam Lindley

Abstract
. Effect handlers allow programmers to model and compose computational effects modularly. Effect systems statically guarantee that all effects are handled. Several recent practical effect systems are based on either row polymorphism or capabilities. However, there remains a gap in understanding the precise relationship between effect systems with such disparate foundations. The main difficulty is that in both row-based and capability-based systems, effect tracking is typically entangled with other features such as functions. We propose a uniform framework for encoding, analysing, and comparing effect systems. Our framework exploits and generalises modal effect types, a recent novel effect system which decouples effect tracking from functions via modalities. Modalities offer fine-grained control over when and how effects are tracked, enabling us to express different strategies for effect tracking. We give encodings as macro translations from existing row-based and capability-based effect systems into our framework and show that these encodings preserve types and semantics. Our encodings reveal the essence of effect tracking mechanisms in different effect systems, enable a direct analysis on their differences, and provide valuable insights on language design.



1

1.
Introduction

Effect handlers
(
Plotkin and Pretnar 2013
)
provide a powerful abstraction to
define and compose computational effects including state, concurrency,
and probability. Effect systems statically ensure that all effects used in a program
are handled.
The literature includes much work on effect systems for effect
handlers
based on a range of different theoretical foundations. Two of the most
popular and well-studied approaches are row-based effect
systems
(
Hillerström and Lindley 2016
;
Leijen 2017
;
Lindley et al. 2017
)
and capability-based effect
systems
(
Brachthäuser et al. 2020
;
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
;
Boruch-Gruszecki et al. 2023
)
.

Row-based effect systems, as in the languages

Koka

(
Leijen 2017
;
Xie et al. 2022
)
,
Links

(
Hillerström and Lindley 2016
)
, and

Frank

(
Lindley et al. 2017
)
, follow the traditional monadic reading of
effects: effects are what computations do when they run.
They treat effect types as a row of effects and annotate each function
arrow with an effect row.
For modularity, they implement
parametric effect polymorphism

via row polymorphism
(
Rémy 1994
;
Leijen 2005
)
.
For example, a standard application function in

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}

(
Xie et al. 2020
)
, a core calculus of
Koka
, has type:

\forall\varepsilon.({\texttt{Int}}\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{Int}}\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{1}}

It is polymorphic in its effects
\varepsilon
, which must agree with the
effect performed by its first argument.

Capability-based effect systems, as in the language

Effekt

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2020
;
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
and an extension to

Scala
3
(
Boruch-Gruszecki et al. 2023
)
, adopt a contextual reading of
effects: effects are capabilities provided by the context.
Treating effects as capabilities enables a notion of
contextual
effect polymorphism

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2020
)
which allows
effect-polymorphic reuse of functions without effect variables.
For example, an uncurried application function in

System C

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
, a core calculus of
Effekt
, has
type:

(\@ifmtarg{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}{{\texttt{Int}}}{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{,{\texttt{Int}}}})\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}

The argument
f
is a capability.
It is a second-class function that cannot be returned as a value.
It can use any capabilities the context provides.
We write
\Rightarrow
for second-class functions.
For a curried application function, which requires returning a
function, we must capture capabilities in types:

(\@ifmtarg{}{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}{\@ifmtarg{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}{}{,f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}})\Rightarrow(\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;\{f\})

Its result has type
(\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;\{f\})
.
As well as specifying an argument and result types as usual, this type
also includes a
capture set

\{f\}
which records that the
returned function may use the capability
f
bound by the argument
type
(f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}})
.

Though row-based and capability-based effect systems are both
well-studied, their relationship is not.
In this paper, we aim to bridge this gap in the literature by
encoding both styles of effect systems into a uniform framework.

Yoshioka et al. 2024
propose a parameterised calculus which can be
instantiated to various row-based effect systems, but they point out
that it is challenging future work to extend their approach to
capability-based effect systems.
Row-based and capability-based effect systems differ significantly in
both theoretical foundations and interpretations of effects.
Moreover, their mechanisms for tracking effects are
entangled

with other features such as functions.
For instance, as we have seen above, a function arrow in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is not
only a standard function type but also provides effect annotations.
Similarly, a function arrow in
System C
may bind capabilities.
The entanglement of effect tracking with such features is the central
challenge in analysing the differences between such effect systems.

An alternative foundation for effect systems has recently emerged in
the form of
modal effect types
(
Met
)
(
Tang et al. 2025
)
, a
novel approach to effect systems based on multimodal type
theory
(
Gratzer et al. 2021
;
Gratzer 2023
;
Kavvos and Gratzer 2023
)
.

Met

decouples
effect tracking from standard type and term
constructs via modalities.
For instance, an application function in
Met
has a plain function
type
({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}
.
This type imposes no restriction on how effects from the context may
be used.
To control the use of effects, we can add modalities to the type.
For example, the type

{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}

restricts the argument function to use only the operation

yield
by wrapping it with the
absolute modality

[{\texttt{yield}}]
(modalities have higher precedence than function
arrows); the type

{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})

restricts both the argument and result functions to be pure.

Tang et al. 2025
focus on the pragmatics of
Met
, especially how
modalities enable concise type signatures to higher-order functions
without losing modularity.
In this paper we exploit the observation that the decoupling of effect
tracking via modalities leads to a tangible increase in flexibility
and expressivity compared to typical effect systems whose effect
tracking is entangled with other features.
Such decoupling allows us to encode a range of effect systems,
including those based on rows and capabilities, in a uniform
framework.

We introduce
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), a System F-style core calculus with modal
effect types parameterised by an
effect theory

\mathcal{X}
.
The effect theory is our main extension to
Met

(
Tang et al. 2025
)
.
An effect theory, inspired by prior work on abstracting row and effect
types
(
Morris and McKinna 2019
;
Hubers and Morris 2023
;
Yoshioka et al. 2024
)
, defines the structure of
effect collections.

Met
hardwires the underlying effect theory to
scoped rows
(
Leijen 2005
)
.
In contrast,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) allows us to smoothly account for the
different treatments of effect collections adopted by different effect
systems, such as sets
(
Bauer and Pretnar 2013
;
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
, simple
rows
(
Morris and McKinna 2019
)
, and scoped rows
(
Leijen 2005
;
Leijen 2017
;
Lindley et al. 2017
)
.
Parameterising by the effect theory enables us to separate the
bureaucracy of managing effect collections from our main concern which
is how to use modalities to encode different effect tracking
mechanisms.

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) has two further extensions to
Met
.
The first extension is
modality-parameterised handlers
.
This is a natural generalisation of effect handlers to be
parameterised by a modality which is used to wrap continuations. This
extension is crucial for the encodings of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
as we
will see in
Section

2.5
.
The second extension is
local labels
, a minimal extension which
allows us to dynamically generate operation
labels
(
de Vilhena and Pottier 2023
)
. This extension is crucial for encoding
named handlers
(
Biernacki et al. 2020
;
Zhang and Myers 2019
;
Brachthäuser et al. 2020
)

(also called lexically-scoped handlers) as adopted in some languages,
especially those with capability-based effect systems like
Effekt
.

As the main novelty of this paper,
we encode, as
macro translations

(
Felleisen 1991
)
, various
effect systems based on rows and capabilities into our uniform
framework
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
We prove that our encodings preserve typing and operational semantics.
Our encodings do not heavily alter the structure of programs but
mostly merely insert terms for manipulating modalities;
our semantics preservation theorems establish a strong correspondence
between the behaviours of source calculi and their translations.
Our primary case studies are encodings of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}

(
Xie et al. 2020
)
, a
core calculus of
Koka
with a row-based effect system, and of

System C

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
, a core calculus of
Effekt
with a
capability-based effect system.
By encoding effect systems into a uniform framework, we can directly
reason about the differences the effect tracking mechanisms of
different effect systems
(
Sections

2.4
and
2.5.4
).
Our encodings also offer practical insights for language designers
(
Section

6.3
).

Beyond analysing differences between effect systems,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) opens
up interesting future research directions. First,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) gives a
uniform intermediate representation for different effect systems which
enables us to design type-directed optimisations without restricting
ourselves to a specific effect system.
Second,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) allows us to design a new effect system by
directly giving its encoding into
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) instead of starting from
scratch. Type soundness and effect safety of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) guarantee the
corresponding properties hold for the new effect system.

The main contributions of this paper are as follows.

•

We give a high-level overview of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) and a high-level
overview of how to encode row-based and capability-based effect
systems into
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) which we use to compare row-based and
capability-based effect systems (
Section

2
).

•

We formally define
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) (
Section

3
)
including our three extensions to
Met
: effect theories,
modality-parameterised handlers, and local labels. We prove type
soundness and effect safety of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) for any effect theory

\mathcal{X}
satisfying certain natural validity conditions.

•

We formally define the encoding of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
, a core calculus with
a row-based effect system à la
Koka
, into
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
) with
the theory
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
for scoped rows
(
Section

4
).
We prove the encoding preserves types and semantics.

•

We formally define the encoding of
System C
, a core calculus
with a capability-based effect system à la
Effekt
, into

Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) with the theory
\mathcal{S}
for sets
(
Section

5
).
We prove the encoding preserves types and semantics.

•

We discuss encodings of further effect systems, insights for
language design provided by our encodings, as well as potential
extensions to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) (
Section

6
).

Section

7
discusses related and future work.
The full specifications and proofs can be found in the supplementary
material.

2.
Overview

In this section we give a high-level overview of the main ideas of the
paper.
We begin with a brief introduction to modal
effects
(
Tang et al. 2025
)
in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) and examples of effect
theories
\mathcal{X}
.
We briefly describe the row-based effect system of

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}

(
Xie et al. 2020
)
and the capability-based effect system of

System C

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
along with their encodings into

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
We use these encodings to directly compare the different systems
in a uniform framework.
We specifically consider encodings of the different kinds of effect
handlers provided by the different systems.
We also briefly discuss the results of encoding other effect systems
in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).

2.1.
Modal Effects and
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
)

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) is a System F-style core calculus.
Every well-typed term in System F is also well-typed in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
For example, we may define a higher-order application function as
follows.

{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\lambda f^{{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}}.\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.f\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}

We use meta-level macros defined by
\doteq
in red to refer to code
snippets.

2.1.1.
Effect Contexts

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) adopts a contextual reading of effects.
Effectful operations are ascribed a type signature, either globally or
locally.
For our examples we begin by assuming global operations
{\texttt{yield}}:{\texttt{Int}}\twoheadrightarrow{\texttt{1}}
and
{\texttt{ask}}:{\texttt{1}}\twoheadrightarrow{\texttt{Int}}
.
Typing judgements include an
ambient effect context
which
tracks the operations that may be performed.
Consider the following function.

\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}}}}

It has type
{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}
.
When applied it performs the
yield
operation using the
{{\mathbsf{do}}}

syntax.
The judgement specifies the effect context with the syntax

\!\!\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}}}}
, which tracks the possibility of
performing
yield
.
We can now apply
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}
to
\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}
and
42
as follows.

\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;(\lambda f^{{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}}.\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.f\;x)~(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x)~42\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}}}}

There is a natural notion of subeffecting on effect contexts.
The following judgement is also valid.

\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}},{\texttt{ask}}}}

2.1.2.
Absolute Modalities

Effect contexts specified by
\!\!\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
belong to typing
judgements instead of types.
As discussed in
Section

1
,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) uses modalities to
track effects in types.
An
absolute modality

[E]
allows us to specify a new
effect context
E
in types different from the ambient one.
For example, consider the following typing derivation.

\frac{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}}}}}{\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen^{\prime}}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}~(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}

This term has type
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})
.
We highlight modalities in blue when they appear in types.
The syntax
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}
introduces an absolute modality

[{\texttt{yield}}]
which specifies a singleton effect context of

yield
and uses it to override the ambient effect context

F
.
The typing judgement of the premise uses the new effect context

yield
as its ambient effect context.
The lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}
tracks the switch of the
effect context and controls the accessibility of variables on the left
of it.
Only variables that are known not to use effects other than

yield
may be used.
This is important to ensure effect safety.
For example, consider the following invalid judgement.

{f:{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\not\vdash}}\;\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}~(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.f\;x)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{ask}}}}}

This program is unsafe as
f
may invoke
ask
which we must
not use under effect context
yield
specified by the modality

[{\texttt{yield}}]
.
The judgement of the function
in this program is as follows.

{f:{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\not\vdash}}\;\;\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.f\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}}}}}

This typing judgement is invalid as the lock

\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}
prevents the use of
f
.
To make it valid, we can annotate the binding of
f
with the modality

[{\texttt{yield}}]
as
f\@ifmtarg{[{\texttt{yield}}]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}}{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}
.
This annotation tracks that the function
f
may only use the
operation
yield
.
Such annotated bindings are introduced by modality elimination.
For instance, we can eliminate the modality of
\text{$\mathit{gen^{\prime}}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}

and then apply it via the
\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}\;\!\!
syntax as follows (where we
elide the typing of the bound term).

\frac{...\quad f\@ifmtarg{[{\texttt{yield}}]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}}{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;f\;42\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}}}}}{\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}\;f={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}~(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x)\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f\;42\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}}}}}

The term
\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x
inside the modality

[{\texttt{yield}}]
is bound to
f
.
The binding of
f
is annotated with this absolute modality.
Consequently, the use of
f
in
f\;42
requires the ambient effect
context to contain the operation
yield
.
In general, whether a variable binding
f\@ifmtarg{\mu}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}}A
can be used
after a lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu}}}
is controlled by a modality
transformation relation which we will introduce in

Section

3.3
.

2.1.3.
Relative Modalities

As well as being able to specify a fresh effect context from scratch
with an absolute modality,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) also has
relative modalities

{\langle D\rangle}
which allow us to extend the ambient effect context with an

extension

D
.
For instance, consider the following derivation.

\frac{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\texttt{yield}}\rangle}}}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;({{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{ask}}\;{()})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{yield}},{\texttt{ask}}}}}{\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\texttt{yield}}\rangle}}~(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;({{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{ask}}\;{()}))\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\texttt{yield}}\rangle}}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\texttt{ask}}}}}

The relative modality
{\langle{\texttt{yield}}\rangle}
extends the ambient effect context

ask
with the operation
yield
.
Consequently, the inside function can use both operations.
Relative modalities are especially useful for giving composable types
to effect handlers.
We refer to
Tang et al. 2025
for further details.
We use relative modalities in the encoding of
System C
as we will see
in
Section

2.3
.

2.1.4.
Effect Theories

Improving on
Met
, we parameterise
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) by an effect theory

\mathcal{X}
which defines the well-formedness relations and equivalence
relations for extensions and effect contexts as well as a subeffecting
relation
E\leqslant F
.
In the remainder of the overview, we will use two effect theories:

\mathcal{S}
, which models effect collections as sets of operations, to
encode capability sets in
System C
, and
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
, which models effect
collections as scoped rows of operations, to encode effect rows in

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
.
Sets are unordered and allow only one occurrence of each label,
whereas scoped rows allow repeated labels and identify rows up to
reordering of non-identical labels.
Both theories support effect variables.
Theory
\mathcal{S}
allows arbitrary numbers of effect variables while
theory
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
only allows at most one effect variable in each row
following row polymorphism
(
Rémy 1994
;
Leijen 2005
)
.

2.2.
Rows as Modal Effects

Koka

(
Leijen 2025
)
has an effect system based on scoped
rows
(
Leijen 2005
)
.

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}

(
Xie et al. 2020
)
is a core calculus underlying
Koka
.
To encode
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
, we use the effect theory
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
of scoped rows.

Function types in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
have the form
A\to^{E}B
, where
E
is
an effect row that specifies the effects that the function may use.
Effect types in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
are entangled with function types.
The key idea of our encoding is to decouple the effect type
E
from
the function arrow via an absolute modality in
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).
Writing
\llbracket-\rrbracket
for translations, we translate a function type as
follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\llbracket A\to^{E}B\rrbracket={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}}(\llbracket A\rrbracket\to\llbracket B\rrbracket)\end{array}

An effectful function in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is decomposed into an absolute modality
and a standard function in
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).
For instance, consider the following first-order effectful function in

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
which invokes the operation
yield
from

Section

2.1
.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\lambda^{{\texttt{yield}}}x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{{\texttt{yield}}}\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\to^{{\texttt{yield}}}{\texttt{1}}\end{array}

(Each
\lambda
-abstraction in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is annotated with an effect row.)
The translation of
\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
is exactly the function

\text{$\mathit{gen^{\prime}}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}
defined in
Section

2.1.2
. We repeat
its definition here for easy reference.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\rrbracket\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}~(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;x)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\end{array}

On the term level, we insert a modality introduction

{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}
for the
\lambda
-abstraction,
corresponding to the type-level modality
[{\texttt{yield}}]
.
We colour
{{{\mathbsf{mod}}}}
in grey in the translations.
The black parts remain terms with valid syntax
and provide intuitions on the translation.
Remember that the modality
{[{\texttt{yield}}]}
is a first-class
type constructor and not part of the function type.

As a more non-trivial example including both higher-order functions
and function application, consider the effect-polymorphic application
function in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
from
Section

1
.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\Lambda\varepsilon^{\mathsf{Effect}}.\lambda f^{{\texttt{Int}}\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{1}}}.\lambda^{\varepsilon}x^{{\texttt{Int}}}.f\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;\forall\varepsilon.({\texttt{Int}}\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{Int}}\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{1}}\end{array}

This function abstracts over an effect variable
\varepsilon
which stands
for the effects performed by the argument
f
.
Both
f
and the inner
\lambda
-abstraction are annotated with

\varepsilon
as
f
is invoked so the effects must match up.
The outer
\lambda
-abstraction is pure as partial application is
pure.
The encoding of
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
in
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
) is as follows.

\begin{array}[]{r@{}c@{}l}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\rrbracket&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}&\text{$\Lambda\varepsilon^{\mathsf{Effect}}$}.{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}}\;(\lambda f^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})}.{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\varepsilon]}}\;(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\varepsilon]}}\;\;f^{\prime}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f^{\prime}\;x))\\
&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}&\forall\varepsilon^{\mathsf{Effect}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}))\end{array}

Each function arrow is associated with an absolute modality reflecting
the effects performed by that function.
For the pure function arrow in the middle, we use the empty absolute
modality
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}
.
The type abstraction
\Lambda\varepsilon
and quantifier
\forall\varepsilon
are
preserved.
We omit kinds when obvious.
In the term, in addition to modality introduction, we also insert a
modality elimination for
f
before applying it to
x
.
The use of
f^{\prime}
requires that the effect variable
\varepsilon
is present
in the effect context.

Our term translation from
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
to
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
) explicitly decouples
the effect tracking mechanism of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
from function abstraction and
application.
This reveals the essence of effect tracking in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
.
Each
\lambda
-abstraction
\lambda^{E}x.M
in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is encoded in

Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
) by inserting a modality introduction

{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}
.
This demonstrates that a function in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
carries its effects.
Each function application
V\;W
in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is encoded by inserting a
modality elimination
\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}\;f=\llbracket V\rrbracket\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f\;\llbracket W\rrbracket

for function
V
of type
A\to^{E}B
.
This demonstrates that when a function is invoked in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
, we need to
provide all effects it may use, as the elimination of
[\llbracket E\rrbracket]

and use of
f
together require
\llbracket E\rrbracket
to be present in the effect
context.

We give the full encoding of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
into
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
) in

Section

4
.

2.3.
Capabilities as Modal Effects

Effekt

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2025
)
has an effect system based on capabilities.

System C

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
is a core calculus underlying

Effekt
.
Since
System C
tracks capabilities as sets, we use the effect theory

\mathcal{S}
of sets to encode it.

Functions in
System C
are called
blocks
.
Blocks are second-class in that they must be fully applied and cannot
be returned.
Capabilities are introduced as block variables.
Unlike row-based effect systems which have a separate notion of
operation labels,
System C
interprets effects as capabilities provided
by the context.
A capability can only be used if it is in scope.

2.3.1.
First-Order Blocks

Let us start with a simple example. Supposing we have a capability

\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}
(for yielding integers) in
the context, we can construct the following block.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:^{\ast}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\{(\@ifmtarg{x:{\texttt{Int}}}{}{x:{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}(x)\}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mid}}\;\;\{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}\}\\
\end{array}

The star
\ast
on the binding of
\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}
indicates that this
block variable is a capability.
Braces delimit blocks. Arguments are wrapped in parentheses. Double
arrows emphasise that blocks are second-class.
The block applies the capability
\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}
from the context to the
argument
x
.
The typing judgement tracks a capability set
\{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}\}
, which
contains all capabilities that the block may use.
The block arrow itself has no capability annotation.
The above block is simply encoded as a
\lambda
-abstraction in

Met
(
\mathcal{S}
).
1
1

1

If we strictly follow the encoding of
System C
in

Section

5.2
, there would be an extra identity modality
for the translated function. This modality is crucial for keeping
the encoding systematic. We omit such identity modalities in the
overview.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}^{\ast}:\mathsf{Effect},\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}),\hat{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}\@ifmtarg{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}}{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}\;\;\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.\hat{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}\;x\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}^{\ast}}}\\
\end{array}

The most interesting aspect of the encoding is how we encode the
capability
{\mathit{y}}:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}
in the context.
A capability
\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}
in
System C
can appear as both a type and a
term.
We introduce an effect variable
{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}
of kind
\mathsf{Effect}
to
represent it at the type level.
We omit kinds in the context when obvious.
We encode the capability
\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}
itself as a term variable of
type
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})
, where the absolute
modality makes sure that whenever
{\mathit{y}}
is invoked the effect
variable
{y}^{\ast}
must be present in the effect context.
To avoid repeatedly writing modality eliminations, the modality of

{\mathit{y}}
is immediately eliminated and bound to
\hat{{\mathit{y}}}
after

{\mathit{y}}
is introduced.
The translation of the block body directly applies
\hat{{\mathit{y}}}
to

x
.
The effect variable
{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}
must be in the effect context
specified by
\!\!\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}}}
because
\hat{{\mathit{y}}}
is used.

2.3.2.
Boxes

In
System C
we can turn a second-class block into a first-class value
by boxing it.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathit{y}}}:^{\ast}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen^{\prime}}$}_{C}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\text{${{\mathbsf{box}}}\;$}\{(\@ifmtarg{x:{\texttt{Int}}}{}{x:{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow{{\mathit{y}}}(x)\}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\text{$\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;$}\{{\mathit{y}}\}\\
\end{array}

This typing judgement has no capability set as it is for values which
are always pure in
System C
.
The value has type
\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\text{$\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;$}\{{\mathit{y}}\}
,
which means it is a boxed block of type
\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}

with capability set
\{{\mathit{y}}\}
.
The block may only use the capability
{\mathit{y}}
.
We can unbox a boxed block
V
via
{{\mathbsf{unbox}}}\;V
which gives back a
second-class block.
We simply encode boxing and unboxing as modality introduction and
elimination in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
).
For instance, we encode
\text{$\mathit{gen^{\prime}}$}_{C}
as follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}^{\ast},\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}),\hat{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}\@ifmtarg{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}}{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen^{\prime}}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}\;\;\text{${\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}$}\;(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.\hat{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}\;x)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\cdot}}\\
\end{array}

The capability set annotation
\!\!\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;\{{\mathit{y}}\}
in the type is encoded
as the absolute modality
[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]
.
The encoding shows the connection between boxes of
System C
and
modalities, supporting the claim of
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
that
boxes of
System C
are inspired by modal
connectives
(
Choudhury and Krishnaswami 2020
)
.

2.3.3.
Higher-Order Blocks

The situation become more involved when we consider higher-order
blocks that take other blocks as arguments.
This is because
System C
entangles the introduction and tracking of
capabilities with blocks, especially their construction and
application.

Let us consider the uncurried and curried application functions
(blocks) introduced in
Section

1
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{}l@{}c@{}l@{}c@{}l}&{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}$}}&\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}&\{(\@ifmtarg{x:{\texttt{Int}}}{f:{\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}}{x:{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{f:{\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}}{}{,f:{\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}}})\Rightarrow f(x)\}&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}&(\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}{}{,f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}})\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}$}}&\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}&\{(\@ifmtarg{}{f:{\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}}{\@ifmtarg{f:{\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}}{}{,f:{\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}}})\Rightarrow{{\mathbsf{box}}}\;\{(\@ifmtarg{x:{\texttt{Int}}}{}{x:{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow f(x)\}\}&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}&(\@ifmtarg{}{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}{\@ifmtarg{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}{}{,f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}})\Rightarrow(\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;\{f\})\\
\end{array}

These are block constructions.
The first block
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
binds the integer parameter
x
first
because
System C
requires value parameters like
x
to appear before
blocks parameters like
f
in a parameter list.
In addition to behaving like standard
\lambda
-abstractions, block
constructions also play an important role in capability tracking.
Specifically:

(1)

Both
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
and
\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
bind a capability

{f:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}
for their block bodies. This
capability
f
can also be used in the type as shown in the type
of
\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
.

(2)

For soundness,
System C
assumes that this new capability
f

is called directly at least once in the block body even if
f
may
actually not be used. (The capability
f
is indeed called
directly in
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
but not so in
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C^{\prime}}
as being
boxed.) Consequently, the capability
f
is always added to the
capability set of the block body tracked by the typing judgement.

(3)

In addition to the new capability
f
, both
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
and

\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
allow any capability from the context to be called
as well.

Our encoding of block constructions in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) takes account of
these three constraints and exposes them explicitly via modalities.
For instance,
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
is encoded as follows.

\begin{array}[]{r@{}c@{}l}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}&\Lambda{f}^{\ast}.{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{f}^{\ast}\rangle}}}~(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.\lambda f^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})}.{{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{f}^{\ast}]}}\;\hat{f}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}\hat{f}\;x)\\[0.86108pt]
&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}&\forall{f}^{\ast}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{f}^{\ast}\rangle}}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{1}})\end{array}

For (1), in order to allow the term variable
f
to appear in types,
we introduce an effect variable
{f}^{\ast}
and wrap the type

{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}
of the argument
f
with an absolute modality

[{f}^{\ast}]
.
The effect variable
{f}^{\ast}
represents the term variable
f
at the
level of types.
Additionally, we immediately eliminate the modality of
f
to

\hat{f}
.
As a result, in the context of the application
\hat{f}\;x
we have
three bindings of
{f}^{\ast}
,
f
, and
\hat{f}
, consistent with the
translation of capability
{\mathit{y}}
as shown in

Section

2.3.1
.
For (2) and (3), we use a relative modality
{\langle{{f}^{\ast}}\rangle}
to
wrap the whole function type.
The relative modality adds the effect variable
{f}^{\ast}
to the
ambient effect context for the function to use, in accordance with
(2).
The relative modality also still allows the function to use effects
from the ambient effect context as we have seen in

Section

2.1.3
, in accordance with (3).

The translation of
\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
is similar.

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}&\Lambda{f}^{\ast}.{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{f}^{\ast}\rangle}}}~(\lambda f^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})}.{{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{f}^{\ast}]}}\;\hat{f}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{f}^{\ast}]}}~(\lambda x.\hat{f}\;x))\\[0.86108pt]
&:&\forall{f}^{\ast}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{f}^{\ast}\rangle}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}))\end{array}

In general, the translation of block types from
System C
to

Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) is as follows, where we let
A
and
B
range over value
types and let
T
range over block types.

\llbracket(\@ifmtarg{\overline{A}}{\overline{f:T}}{\overline{A}\@ifmtarg{\overline{f:T}}{}{,\overline{f:T}}})\Rightarrow B\rrbracket\;=\;\forall\overline{{f}^{\ast}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\overline{{f}^{\ast}}\rangle}}}}(\overline{\llbracket A\rrbracket}\to\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket)

A block type is decomposed into a standard function type with extra
modalities and type quantifiers, which makes explicit exactly how

System C
introduces and tracks capabilities.

2.3.4.
Block Calls

Blocks must be fully applied.
Assuming we have a capability
{\mathit{y}}:\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}
in
the context, we can apply the blocks
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
and

\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
to the block
\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}
as follows.

\begin{array}[]{l@{}l@{}c@{}l@{}c@{}l}{\mathit{y}}:^{\ast}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}&{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}$}}({\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}},42)&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}&{\texttt{1}}&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mid}}&\{{\mathit{y}}\}\\[2.15277pt]
{\mathit{y}}:^{\ast}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}&{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}$}}({\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}})&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}&\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\text{$\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;$}\{{\mathit{y}}\}&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mid}}&\{{\mathit{y}}\}\end{array}

(As blocks must be fully applied, we must additionally pass an integer
to
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
— in this case
42
.)
These are block calls.
Similar to block constructions, block calls in
System C
not only pass
arguments to a block but also play an important role in capability
tracking.
Specifically:

(1)

Recall that both
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
and
\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
bind a
capability
f
. Consequently, when calling them with

\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}
,
System C
substitutes
f
with the capability set

\{{\mathit{y}}\}
of
\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}
in types. This is reflected by

\!\!\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;\{{\mathit{y}}\}
in the type of calling
\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
(before
substitution it was
\!\!\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;\{f\}
).

(2)

Recall that
System C
assumes the capability
f
bound by

\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
and
\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
is called directly.
Consequently, the capability set of the whole block call must be
extended with the capability set
\{{\mathit{y}}\}
of the argument

\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}
.
This is reflected by the fact that both typing judgements track the
capability sets
\{{\mathit{y}}\}
even though the application of

\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
does not call
{\mathit{y}}
directly.

Our encoding of block calls in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) takes account of these two
constraints and exposes them explicitly via modalities.
For instance, our example application of
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
is encoded as
follows.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}^{\ast},\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}),\hat{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}\@ifmtarg{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}}{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\\
\hskip 60.00009pt\text{${{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}\rangle}}}\;$}f=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket\;{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f\;42\;({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}\;\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}}}\end{array}

For (1), recall that in the translation
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket
we bind
an effect variable
{f}^{\ast}
to represent the capability
f
and wrap
the argument type with an absolute modality
[{f}^{\ast}]
.
Thus for the application of
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket
, we instantiate the
effect variable
{f}^{\ast}
with
{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}
and box the argument

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket

with the absolute modality
[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]
.
For (2), the elimination of the relative modality
{\langle{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}\rangle}

of
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket\;{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}
and the use of
f
ensure that

{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}
must be present in the effect context.

The translation of the call of
\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
is similar.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}^{\ast},\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}),\hat{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}\@ifmtarg{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}}{\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\\
\hskip 60.00009pt\text{${{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}\rangle}}}\;$}f=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket\;{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f\;({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}\;\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}}}\end{array}

As with the encoding of
Section

2.2
, the
encoding of
System C
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) helps elucidate exactly how the
capability tracking of
System C
is entangled with constructs like
block constructions and calls.
Modality introduction and elimination reveal the hidden mechanisms.

We give the full encoding of
System C
into
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) in

Section

5
.

2.4.
Comparing Rows and Capabilities

As a uniform framework,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) allows us to directly compare the how
effect tracking differs in different effect systems without dealing
with the subtleties in their typing and reduction rules.

For instance, let us compare the encoding of function types and
polymorphic types in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
with the encoding of block types and box
types in
System C
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{~}r@{~}c@{~}l}\text{{{System~$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}} to {{Met}}(${\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}$) : }&\llbracket A\to^{E}B\rrbracket&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}}(\llbracket A\rrbracket\to\llbracket B\rrbracket)\\[2.15277pt]
&\llbracket\forall\varepsilon.A\rrbracket&=&\forall\varepsilon.\llbracket A\rrbracket\\[2.15277pt]
\text{{{System~C}} to {{Met}}($\mathcal{S}$) : }&\llbracket(\@ifmtarg{\overline{A}}{\overline{f:T}}{\overline{A}\@ifmtarg{\overline{f:T}}{}{,\overline{f:T}}})\Rightarrow B\rrbracket&=&\forall\overline{{f}^{\ast}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\overline{{f}^{\ast}}\rangle}}}}(\overline{\llbracket A\rrbracket}\to\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket)\\[2.15277pt]
&\llbracket T\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;C\rrbracket&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket\end{array}

From the encodings we can immediately observe two key differences
of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
.

(1)

The encoding of function types in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is wrapped with an absolute
modality, whereas the encoding of a block type in
System C
is wrapped
with a relative modality.
The encoding of box types in
System C
is wrapped with an absolute
modality.
The different modalities reveal a fundamental difference between the
meanings of functions in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and blocks in
System C
: functions in

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
can only use those effects specified in their types, whereas
blocks in
System C
can use arbitrary effects from the context unless
they are boxed.

(2)

The encoding of block types in
System C
binds a list of effect
variables and wraps each block argument type with an absolute modality
of the corresponding effect variable, whereas the encoding of a
function type in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is much less involved.
Only the encoding of polymorphic types in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
binds effect
variables.
The difference in the treatment of argument types reveals that
capabilities in
System C
act as an implicit form of parametric
polymorphism, abstracting the capabilities used by each block
variable.
This explains why capability-based effect systems do not require
explicit effect variables in many cases where row-based effect systems
do.

2.5.
Encoding Effect Handlers

We have seen how effectful functions in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
are encoded
in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
These are the most important parts of our encodings, as most effect
systems track effects by giving different intepretations to functions.
Though all effect systems discussed in this paper support effect
handlers, the same ideas apply equally to traditional effect systems
for languages with only built-in effects.
Nonetheless, the encodings of effect handlers in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
are interesting and reveal fundamental differences between the typing
and semantics of effect handlers in these two calculi.
In this section, we first briefly review what effect handlers are and
then show how effect handlers in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
are encoded.

2.5.1.
Effect Handlers in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
)

Effect handlers allow us to customise how to handle effectful
operations.
For instance, we can write a handler to handle the
yield

operation defined in
Section

2.1
by summing up all
yielded integers as follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;({{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;42;{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{{\texttt{yield}}}\;37;0)\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{\texttt{yield}}\;p\;r\mapsto p+r\;{()}\}\end{array}

The computation
{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;42;{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;37;0
is handled by
the handler
\{{\texttt{yield}}\;p\;r\mapsto p+r\;{()}\}
.
The handler consists of one operation clause for the operation

yield
.
In this operation clause, the variable
p
of type
Int
is bound to
the parameter of the
yield
operation, and the variable
r
of
type
{\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}}
is bound to its recursively-handled
continuation.
(This kind of recursive handling is known as
deep
handlers

(
Kammar et al. 2013
)
in the literature.)
For instance, when the first
yield
operation is handled,
p

is
42
and
r
is the continuation
\lambda y^{\texttt{1}}.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;({{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;37;0)\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{\texttt{yield}}\;p\;r\mapsto p+r\;{()}\}
.
The handler clause adds the yielded integer
p
to the result of the
continuation
r
, thus returning the sum of all handled operations.
The above program reduces to
79
.
Effect handlers also have a return clause which we omit here, but
describe in
Section

3
.

2.5.2.
Encoding Effect Handlers in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
does not use the
{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\!\!
syntax.
Instead, a handler in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is defined as a handler value, which is a
function that takes an argument to handle.
Consider the following polymorphic handler for the
yield

operation.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\Lambda\varepsilon.\text{${{\mathbsf{handler}}}$}\;\{{\texttt{yield}}\;p\;r\mapsto p+r\;{()}\}\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;\forall\varepsilon.({\texttt{1}}\to^{{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon}{\texttt{Int}})\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{Int}}\end{array}

The term
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
is polymorphic over other effects

\varepsilon
that it does not handle.
The
{{\mathbsf{handler}}}
syntax defines a handler, which is a function that
takes an argument of type
{\texttt{1}}\to^{{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon}{\texttt{Int}}
,
calls this argument with unit and handles the
yield

operation.
The continuation
r
has type
{\texttt{1}}\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{Int}}
as it may
use effects abstracted by
\varepsilon
.
For instance, we can apply
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
as follows which
reduces to
79
.

{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\;E\;(\lambda x^{{\texttt{1}}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;42;{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;37;0)

We can easily encode
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
in
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
) as a
polymorphic function whose function body uses the
{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\!\!

syntax to handle the argument.
The main difficulty is that for the handler clause, the continuation

r
should have type
\llbracket{\texttt{1}}\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{Int}}\rrbracket={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}})
following the translation of
function types in
Section

2.2
.
However, the typing rule of handlers in
Tang et al. 2025
only
allows us to give a function type to
r
with no modality.
To solve this problem, we introduce
modality-parameterised
handlers
.
In
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), the handler syntax is annotated with a modality
\mu

as
{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H
.
The continuation
r
in the handler clause of
H
now has type

{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}(A\to B)
for some types
A
and
B
.
With the modality-parameterised handler, we can translate

\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
as follows, omitting the details of the
translation of the handler clause, which we name
H^{\prime}
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{}c@{}l}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\rrbracket&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}&\Lambda\varepsilon.{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\varepsilon]}}~(\lambda f^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}})}.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{[\varepsilon]}\;({{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon]}}\;f^{\prime}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f\;{()})\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H^{\prime})\\
&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}&\forall\varepsilon.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\varepsilon]}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}})\to{\texttt{Int}})\end{array}

We eliminate the modality of the argument
f
before applying and
handling it.
The type translation follows the translation given in

Section

2.2
.
We give full details of our modality-parameterised handlers in

Section

3.5
and formally define the translation of handlers
in
Section

4.2
.

2.5.3.
Encoding Effect Handlers in
System C

System C
adopts named handlers.
Instead of using operation labels to identify which operation we want
to invoke and handle, in
System C
each handler binds a fresh
capability in the scope of the handler and handles the use of this
capability.
For instance, we can define a named handler and use it to handle a
computation as follows.

\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\text{${{\mathbsf{try}}}$}\;\{{{\mathit{y}}}^{{\texttt{Int}}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}\Rightarrow{\mathit{y}}(42);{\mathit{y}}(37);0\}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{p\;r\mapsto p+r({()})\}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mid}}\;\;C

Handlers in
System C
use the
{{\mathbsf{try}}}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\!\!
syntax.
This handler introduces a capability
{\mathit{y}}
of type

\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}
in the scope between
{{\mathbsf{try}}}
and

\!\!\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\!\!
.
We use the capability
{\mathit{y}}
to yield integers
42
and
37
.
These two uses of
y
are handled by the handler, whose operation
clause is similar to what we have seen before, except it uses a
capability in place of an operation label.

The semantics of named handlers in
System C
differs from that of the
standard effect handlers of
Plotkin and Pretnar 2013
.
Named handlers have a generative semantics
(
Biernacki et al. 2020
)

which dynamically generates a fresh runtime label for each capability
introduced by a handler.
Dynamic generation guarantees the uniqueness of runtime labels, which
ensures that all uses of a capability must be handled by the handler
that introduces the capability.

To encode the named handlers of
System C
into
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), we need to
resolve this semantic gap.
Adding named handlers to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) would work but is rather heavyweight.
We observe that the essence of named handlers is actually a way to
dynamically generate labels.
We introduce
local labels
to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), which decouple
dynamic generation of labels from named handlers.
This extension is inspired by the local effects of

Biernacki et al. 2019
, dynamic labels of
de Vilhena and Pottier 2023
, and
fresh labels of the
Links
language
(
Jourde 2022
)
.
The syntax
{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M
introduces a local label
in the scope of
M
.
The type system ensures the local label
\ell
cannot escape from
M
.
The semantics generates a fresh label to replace the local label

\ell
.
We provide the details in
Section

3
.
With local labels, we can encode
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}
as follows, omitting
the handler
H^{\prime}
, which contains an operation clause for
\ell_{\mathit{y}}

translated from the handler of
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}
.

\hskip-5.0pt\begin{array}[]{l@{}r@{}c@{}l}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}&\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}&{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell_{\mathit{y}}:{\texttt{Int}}\twoheadrightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}\\[2.15277pt]
&\hfil\hfil\hfil\quad(\lambda\text{${\mathit{y}}$}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\text{$\ell_{\mathit{y}}$}]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{1}})}.{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{\mathit{y}}]}}\;\hat{{\mathit{y}}}={\mathit{y}}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\hat{{\mathit{y}}}\;42;\hat{{\mathit{y}}}\;37;0)\;({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{\mathit{y}}]}}\;(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{\mathit{y}}\;x))\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H^{\prime}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{:}}\;{\texttt{Int}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\llbracket C\rrbracket}}\end{array}

We introduce a local label
\ell_{\mathit{y}}
for the handler.
We use the term
{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{\mathit{y}}]}}\;(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{\mathit{y}}\;x)
which invokes the operation
\ell_{\mathit{y}}
to simulate the
capability introduced by the named handler in
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}
.
The translation of the handled computation binds this function to

{\mathit{y}}
, eliminates the modality of
{\mathit{y}}
to
\hat{{\mathit{y}}}
, and uses

\hat{{\mathit{y}}}
to yield integers
42
and
37
.
As in the encoding of effect handlers in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
, we also use our
modality-parameterised handlers here and annotate
{{\mathbsf{handle}}}
with the modality

[\llbracket C\rrbracket]
.

Our translation
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket
is simplified for clarity; it is
actually the result of reducing the full translation of
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}

by a few steps.
We give the full translation in
Section

5.2
.

2.5.4.
Comparing Encodings of Effect Handlers

Our encodings of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
effect handlers elucidate how
effect handlers differ in these two effect systems.

(1)

The
System C
encoding requires local labels, whereas the
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
encoding does not, which reveals the syntactic difference that
capabilities in
System C
have scopes whereas operation labels in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
do not, and the semantic difference that
System C
generates fresh
runtime labels for effect handlers, whereas
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
does not.

(2)

The
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
encoding performs operations directly, whereas the

System C
encoding wraps operation invocations into a function (such as
the term
{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{\mathit{y}}]}}(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{\mathit{y}}\;x)

in
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}$}}\rrbracket
) and passes this function to the handled
computation.
This difference shows how in a capability-based effect system such as

System C
operations are not directly invoked via their labels but are
instead invoked and passed around as blocks explicitly at the term
level.

2.6.
More Encodings

The encodings of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
illustrate the core idea of using
modalities to encode and compare effect systems with different
foundations in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
However,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) can be used for much more than encoding
these two effect systems.
In
Section

6
, we will discuss two more representative
encodings of effect systems into
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), including

•

System
\Xi

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2020
)
, an early core calculus for
Effekt
based on capabilities, and

•

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}

(
Xie et al. 2022
)
, a core calculus formalising
scope-safe named handlers of
Koka
.

These results further demonstrate the expressiveness of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) as
a general framework to encode, compare, and analyse effect systems.
We discuss insights we learn from our encodings in
Section

6.3
.

3.
The Core Calculus
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
)

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) is a System F-style call-by-value core calculus and modal
effect types parameterised by an effect theory
\mathcal{X}
.
In addition to the effect theory,
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) also extends
Met
with local labels and modality-parameterised handlers.
We aim to be self-contained about modal effect types in this paper and
refer to
Tang et al. 2025
for a more complete introduction.

3.1.
Syntax

The syntax of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) is as follows.
We highlight syntax relevant to modal effect types and our extensions
of local labels and modality-parameterised handlers in grey.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Types}&A,B&::=&{\texttt{1}}\mid A\to B\mid\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A$}}}\mid{\forall\alpha^{K}.A}\\
\textrm{Modalities}&\mu,\nu&::=&\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle[E]$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle[E]$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle[E]$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle[E]$}}}\mid\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle{\langle D\rangle}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle{\langle D\rangle}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle{\langle D\rangle}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle D\rangle}$}}}\\
\textrm{Extensions}&D&::=&\cdot\mid\ell,D\mid\varepsilon,D\\
\textrm{Effect Contexts}&E,F&::=&\cdot\mid\ell,E\mid\varepsilon,E\\
\textrm{Kinds}&K&::=&{\mathsf{Abs}}\mid{\mathsf{Any}}\mid\mathsf{Effect}\\
\textrm{Contexts}&\Gamma&::=&\cdot\mid\Gamma,\alpha:K\mid\Gamma,\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\mu}_{E}}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\mu}_{E}}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\mu}_{E}}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\mu}_{E}}}}$}}}\\
&&\mid&\Gamma,\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}}}A$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}}}A$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}}}A$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}}}A$}}}\mid\Gamma,{\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B}\\
\textrm{Label Contexts}&\Sigma&::=&\cdot\mid\Sigma,{\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B}\\
\end{array}\hfill\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Terms}&M,N&::=&{()}\mid x\mid\lambda x^{A}.M\mid M\,N\\
&&\mid&\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\mid M\;A\mid\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,V$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,V$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,V$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,V$}}}\\
&&\mid&\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}\\
&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;M\mid\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M$}}}\\
&&\mid&\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H$}}}\\
\textrm{Values}&V,W&::=&{()}\mid x\mid\lambda x^{A}.M\mid\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\mid{{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,V}\\
&&\mid&V\;A\mid\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;W\\
\textrm{Handlers}&H&::=&\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N,\ell\;p\;r\mapsto M\}\\
\end{array}

We have two kinds
\mathsf{Abs}
and
\mathsf{Any}
for value types and one kind

\mathsf{Effect}
for extensions and effect contexts.
By convention, we usually write
\alpha
for type variables of values
and
\varepsilon
for effect variables.
We omit kinds when obvious.
We let
A
range over both value types
A
and effect contexts
E
,
and let
\alpha
range over type variables for them in type
abstraction
\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V
and type application
M\;A
.

Unlike
Tang et al. 2025
, we omit masking, as it is not used by our
encodings.
We discuss future extensions to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), including masking, in

Section

6.4
.

For simplicity, we assume that each handler only handles one
operation, and fix a global context
\Sigma
which associates each
global operation label with its type.
An entry
\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B
indicates that the operation
\ell
takes
an argument of type
A
and returns a value of type
B
.
We also support local labels which are introduced by

{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M
and maintained in the context
\Gamma
.
We do not distinguish between local and global labels syntactically.

Values include type application and modality elimination whose
subterms are restricted to be values, following the notion of

complex values
in call-by-push-value
(
Levy 2004
)
.
Such complex values are convenient as we adopt a value
restriction
(
Wright 1995
)
for type abstraction and modality
introduction.

3.2.
Effect Theories

An effect theory defines the structure of effect collections, that is,
extensions and effect contexts in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
Extensions
D
and effect contexts
E
are both syntactically defined
as lists of labels and effect variables.
We overload commas for list concatenation, e.g.,
D,E
and
E,F
are
both list concatenation.
As usual, list concatenation is associative but not commutative.
The kinding, equivalence, and subtyping (or subeffecting) relations
for them are determined by an effect theory
\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 3.1 (Effect theory).

An effect theory
\mathcal{X}
is a tuple
\langle:,\equiv\rangle

of two relations.

•

\Gamma\vdash D:\mathsf{Effect}
is a kinding relation which
defines well-formed extensions and is preserved by concatenation

D,D^{\prime}
. That is, if
\Gamma\vdash D:\mathsf{Effect}
and
\Gamma\vdash D^{\prime}:\mathsf{Effect}
, then
\Gamma\vdash D,D^{\prime}:\mathsf{Effect}
.

•

\Gamma\vdash D\equiv D^{\prime}
is an equivalence relation for
well-formed extensions.

Our definition of an effect theory
\mathcal{X}
is minimal and only includes
the definitions of kinding and equivalence relations for extensions

D
. We can naturally derive the kinding relation
\Gamma\vdash E:\mathsf{Effect}
, equivalence relation
\Gamma\vdash E\equiv E^{\prime}
, and
subeffecting relation
\Gamma\vdash E\leqslant E^{\prime}
for effect contexts
as follows.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad\quad}l@{\qquad\quad}l@{\qquad\quad}l}\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\cdot:\mathsf{Effect}}\par&\frac{\Gamma\ni\varepsilon:\mathsf{Effect}}{\Gamma\vdash\varepsilon:\mathsf{Effect}}\par&\frac{\Gamma\vdash D:\mathsf{Effect}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma\vdash E:\mathsf{Effect}\hskip 8.19447pt}{\Gamma\vdash D,E:\mathsf{Effect}}\\[2.15277pt]
\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\cdot\equiv\cdot}\par&\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon}\par&\frac{\Gamma\vdash D_{1}\equiv D_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma\vdash E_{1}\equiv E_{2}}{\Gamma\vdash D_{1},E_{1}\equiv D_{2},E_{2}}\par&\frac{\Gamma\vdash E,E^{\prime}\equiv F\hskip 8.19447pt}{\Gamma\vdash E\leqslant F}\end{array}

The kinding and equivalence relations for effect contexts are defined
inductively.
The subeffecting relation is more interesting.
We have
E\leqslant F
if there exists an effect context
E^{\prime}
such that

E,E^{\prime}
is well-formed and
E,E^{\prime}\equiv F
.
It is easy to verify that this subeffecting relation is a preorder.
We often write
:_{\mathcal{X}}
,
\leqslant_{\mathcal{X}}
, and
\equiv_{\mathcal{X}}
to denote
which specific effect theory we refer to.
We sometimes omit the context
\Gamma
for the equivalence and
