subeffecting for brevity.

We give three examples of effect theories, among which
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}

and
\mathcal{S}
are used for the encoding of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
in

Sections

4.2
and
5.2
, respectively.

Definition 3.2 (Simple Rows).

{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{simp}}}=\langle:_{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{simp}}},\equiv_{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{simp}}}\rangle
defines
effect collections as simple rows
(
Morris and McKinna 2019
)
of operation labels.
Well-formed extensions consist of distinct labels without any effect
variable.

D\equiv D^{\prime}
if
D
is identical to
D^{\prime}
modulo reordering of
labels.

Definition 3.3 (Scoped Rows).

{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}=\langle:_{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}},\equiv_{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}\rangle
defines
effect collections as scoped rows
(
Leijen 2005
)
of operation
labels.
Well-formed extensions comprise potentially duplicated labels
without any effect variable.

D\equiv D^{\prime}
if
D
is identical to
D^{\prime}
modulo reordering of
distinct labels.

Definition 3.4 (Sets).

\mathcal{S}=\langle:_{\mathcal{S}},\equiv_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
defines effect
collections as sets.
Well-formed extensions are sets of labels and effect variables.
The equivalence relation is set equivalence.

Full formal definitions of these effect theories are given in

Appendix

A
.
The effect theory
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
corresponds to the treatment of effect
collections as scoped rows used in
Met
, modulo the fact that
Met
has
presence types for labels in effect contexts, whereas we choose not
to for simplicity.
We discuss extending
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) with presence types and richer effect
kinds in
Section

6.4
.

Following
Yoshioka et al. 2024
, an effect theory that intuitively
characterises the notion of a collection of effects should satisfy the
following validity conditions.

Definition 3.5 (Validity Conditions).

Validity conditions for an effect theory
\mathcal{X}
are

(1)

if
E\leqslant_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot
then
E=\cdot
, and

(2)

if
\ell\leqslant_{\mathcal{X}}\ell^{\prime},E
and
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
then

\ell\leqslant_{\mathcal{X}}E
.

The validity conditions together ensure that if a label
\ell
is a
subtype of an effect context
E
, then it must syntactically appear in
the effect context
E
.
The first condition prevents us from claiming that some label is
contained in the empty effect context.
The second condition prevents us from identifying two syntactically
different label as the same one.
All effect theories given above satisfy the validity conditions.
Our type soundness and effect safety theorems in

Section

3.7
are parameterised by any effect theory
satisfying the validity conditions.

3.3.
Modalities

Modalities manipulate effect contexts as follows.

[E](F)~=~E\qquad\qquad\qquad{\langle D\rangle}(F)~=~D,F\vskip-2.39996pt

The absolute modality
[E]
completely replaces the effect context

F
with
E
.
The extension modality
{\langle D\rangle}
extends the effect context
F
with
D
.
Following
Met

(
Tang et al. 2025
)
, we write
\mu_{F}
for the pair of
modality
\mu
and effect context
F
where
F
is the effect context
that
\mu
manipulates.

Modality Composition

We define the composition of modalities as follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mu\circ{[E]}=[E]\qquad\qquad[E]\circ{{\langle D\rangle}}=[D,E]\qquad\qquad{\langle D_{1}\rangle}\circ{\langle D_{2}\rangle}={\langle D_{2},D_{1}\rangle}\end{array}

Composition is from left to right, for consistency with
Met
.
First, an absolute modality fully determines the new effect context

E
no matter what
\mu
does before.
Second, setting the effect context to
E
followed by extending
E

with
{D}
is equivalent to directly setting the effect context to

D,E
.
Third, consecutive extensions can be composed into one by combining
the extensions.
Composition is well-defined as we have
(\mu\circ\nu)(E)=\nu(\mu(E))
.
We also have associativity
(\mu\circ\nu)\circ\xi=\mu\circ(\nu\circ\xi)
and identity
{\langle\rangle}\circ\mu=\mu\circ{\langle\rangle}=\mu
. All of these properties are independent of the effect theory
\mathcal{X}
.

Modality Transformation

We define a modality transformation judgement, which determines the
coercion of modalities, controlling the accessibility of variables as
mentioned in
Section

2.1.2
.
Given a variable binding
f\@ifmtarg{\mu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}A
(which means
f
is
introduced by eliminating the modality
\mu
of some value of type

{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A
at effect context
F
), we can access it after a lock

\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu_{F}}}}
if the modality
transformation relation
\Gamma\vdash\mu\Rightarrow\nu\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
holds.
The modality transformation judgement is defined as follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash E\leqslant\mu(F)\hskip 8.19447pt}{\Gamma\vdash[E]\Rightarrow{\mu}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\quad\textsc{left=\text{{MT-Abs}}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash D_{1},F\leqslant D_{2},F\text{ for all }E\leqslant F}{\Gamma\vdash{\langle D_{1}\rangle}\Rightarrow{\langle D_{2}\rangle}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{left=\text{{MT-Extend}}}\end{array}

Both rules make sure that we do not lose any effects after
transformation.
Rule
MT-Abs
allows us to transform an absolute modality

[E]
to any other modality
\mu
as long as
E\leqslant\mu(F)
.
Rule
MT-Extend
allows us to transform an extension modality

{\langle D_{1}\rangle}
to another extension modality
{\langle D_{2}\rangle}
as long as for
any effect context
F
larger than
E
, we have
D_{1},F\leqslant D_{2},F
.
We need to quantify over all effect contexts
F
which are larger than
the ambient effect context
E
because the new effect context that a
relative modality gives us depends on the ambient effect context.
We need to make sure the transformation is safe up to upcasts of the
ambient effect context.

Our
MT-Extend
rule is suitable for any effect theory, whereas
the corresponding rule
MT-Upcast
in
Tang et al. 2025
is
specific to the treatment of effect collections as scoped rows in

Met
.
Given a specific effect theory, we can usually find an
easier-to-compute representation of
MT-Extend
without universal
quantification (as is the case for the
MT-Upcast
rule in
Met
).

3.4.
Kinds and Contexts

The kinding relations for extensions and effect contexts are provided
by the effect theory
\mathcal{X}
in

Section

3.2
.
For value types, we have two kinds where
\mathsf{Abs}
is a subkind of

\mathsf{Any}
.
A type has kind
\mathsf{Abs}
if all function types appearing as syntactic
subterms of the type are wrapped in absolute modalities.
For example,
({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{1}}
does not have kind
\mathsf{Abs}

whereas
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}(({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{1}})\to{\texttt{1}})
does.
Intuitively, values whose types have kind
\mathsf{Abs}
do not depend on the
ambient effect context.
For any operation
\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B
, types
A
and
B
should have kind

\mathsf{Abs}
to avoid effect leakage following
Tang et al. 2025
.
The kinding and type equivalence rules of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) are given in

Appendix

B
.

Contexts are ordered.
We have
\Gamma\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
if the context
\Gamma
is well-formed at
effect context
E
.
For instance,
the following context is well-formed at effect context
E
.

{x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}A_{1},y\@ifmtarg{\nu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu_{F}}}}}A_{2}},~{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}}},~{z\@ifmtarg{\xi_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\xi_{E}}}}}A_{3},w:A_{4}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}

Let us read from right to left.
Variable
w
is at effect context
E
(it is technically tagged with
an identity modality which is omitted).
Variable
z
is tagged with modality
\xi_{E}
, which means it is not at
effect context
E
but actually at effect context
\xi(E)
.
Lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}}
changes the effect context to
E
from

F
. We go back to
F
.
Variables
x
and
y
are at effect contexts
\mu(F)
and
\nu(F)
,
respectively.
Each modality in the context carries an index of the effect context it
manipulates, making switching of effect contexts explicit.
We frequently omit the index when it is clear what it must be.
Formal definitions of kinding and context well-formedness rules are in

Section

B.1
.
We define
\mathsf{locks}(-)
to compose all the modalities on
the locks in a context.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{locks}(\cdot)={{\langle\rangle}}\qquad\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}})=\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma)\circ{\mu}\qquad\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma,x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}}}A)=\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma)\\
\end{array}

We identify contexts up to the following two equations.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\rangle}_{F}}}},\Gamma^{\prime}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}=\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\qquad\qquad\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\mu}_{F}}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\nu}_{F^{\prime}}}}},\Gamma^{\prime}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}=\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{({\mu}\circ{\nu})_{F}}}},\Gamma^{\prime}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\\
\end{array}

3.5.
Typing

Figure

1
gives the typing rules for
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
As before, we highlight rules relevant to modal effect types and our
extensions in grey.
The typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
means that the term
M

has type
A
under context
\Gamma
and effect context
E
with
well-formedness condition
\Gamma\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
.

Figure 1
.
Typing rules and auxiliary rules of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).

Modality Introduction and Elimination

Rule
T-Mod
introduces a modality
\mu
to the conclusion, puts a
lock into the context of the premise, and changes the effect context.
Rule
T-Letmod
eliminates a modality
\mu
and moves it to the
variable binding.
We have seen examples that rely on these rules in

Section

2.1
.
There is another modality
\nu
in
T-Letmod
which is needed for
technical reasons to support sequential elimination.
For instance, given a variable
x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A
with
two modalities, to eliminate both
\nu
and
\mu
, we can first
eliminate
\nu
to
y\@ifmtarg{\nu}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A
and then to

z\@ifmtarg{\nu\circ\mu}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu\circ\mu}}}}A
as follows.

\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\nu}\;y=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;z=y\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M

We restrict
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}
and
\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\!\!
to values to avoid
effect leakage, as in
Met

(
Tang et al. 2025
;
Lorenzen et al. 2024
)
.

Accessing Variables

Locks control the accessibility of variables as we have shown in

Section

2.1
.
Rule
T-Var
uses the auxiliary judgement

\Gamma\vdash(\mu,A)\Rightarrow\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
(also defined in

Figure

1
) to check whether we can access a variable

x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}A
given all locks in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
When
A
has kind
\mathsf{Abs}
, we can always use
x
as it does not depend
on the effect context.
Otherwise we need to make sure the coercion from
\mu
to

\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})
is safe by checking the modality transformation
relation
\Gamma\vdash\mu\Rightarrow\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
where

\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})
composes the modalities on locks in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
We have seen an example in
Section

2.1.2
.
As another example,
x\@ifmtarg{{\langle\ell\rangle}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\ell\rangle}}}}}{\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{1}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\ell^{\prime}]}}}\vdash x:{\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{1}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\ell^{\prime}}}
is
ill-typed since we cannot transform the modality
{\langle\ell\rangle}
to

[\ell^{\prime}]
.
It would be well-typed if
x
had type
Int
.

Local Labels

Rule
T-LocalEffect
binds a local label
\ell
with type
signature
A\twoheadrightarrow B
.
This label
\ell
appears in the context of the typing judgement for
term
M
.
Well-formedness of type
A^{\prime}
and effect context
E
under
\Gamma

prevents
\ell
from appearing in
A^{\prime}
and
E
.
Rule
T-Do
may use any label from
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
.
The operational semantics (
Section

3.6
) generates
runtime labels to substitute local labels.

Modality-Parameterised Handlers

Rule
T-Handle
defines a handler and uses it to handle a
computation
M
.
Let us first ignore all occurrences of the modality
\mu
.
A handler of operation
\ell
extends the effect context with
\ell

as indicated by the lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\ell\rangle}_{E}}}}
in the typing
judgement of
M
.
The return value of
M
is bound to the variable
x
in the return
clause
{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N
.
The type of
x
also has the modality
{\langle\ell\rangle}
since
x
may use
the operation
\ell
, e.g., when
M
returns a function
\lambda x.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;x
.

We generalise the handlers of
Tang et al. 2025
to be parameterised
by a modality
\mu
.
The modality
\mu
transforms the effect context
F
to
\mu(F)=E

for the whole term as witnessed by the addition of the lock

\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}
to the context of each premise.
Since both the handled computation and the handler clauses are
well-typed under the lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}
, we can wrap the
continuation
r
, which captures the handled computation and the
handler, into the modality
\mu
.
The return value
x
is also wrapped in the modality
\mu
as it is
from
M
.
This is in contrast to the handler rule of
Tang et al. 2025
, as
shown below, which just gives
r
the function type
B^{\prime}\to B
.

{\frac{\Sigma,\Gamma\ni\ell:A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow B^{\prime}\quad\ \Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{{\langle\ell\rangle}}_{E}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\ell,E}}\quad\ \Gamma,x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\ell\rangle}}}}A\vdash N:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad\ \Gamma,p:A^{\prime},r:B^{\prime}\to B\vdash N^{\prime}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N,\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N^{\prime}\}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}}

To recover the original handler construct of
Tang et al. 2025
, we
just need to instantiate the modality
\mu
to the identity modality

{\langle\rangle}
as shown by the following syntactic sugar.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N,\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N^{\prime}\}\\
&\doteq&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{{\langle\rangle}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N,\ell\;p\;r\mapsto\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}\;r=r\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N^{\prime}\}\\
\end{array}

Having a modality
\mu
for the continuation
r
allows us to have
more fine-grained control over effect tracking for the continuation.
As discussed in
Section

2.5
, the extra expressiveness
provided by this rule is especially useful for a unified framework to
encode other effect systems with support for effect handlers, as
different encodings typically require translating a function type into
a type with some modalities.
We give an example of an effect handler annotated with the empty
absolute modality
[]
in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) based on the handler

\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}
in
Section

2.5.1
.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{[]}\;({{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{\texttt{yield}}\;42;{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;{{\texttt{yield}}}\;37;0)\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}}]}\;x^{\prime}=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x^{\prime},\\
{\texttt{yield}}\;p\;r\mapsto\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\;r^{\prime}=r\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;p+r^{\prime}\;{()}\}\end{array}\end{array}

As a result of the annotation
[]
, the continuation
r
has type

{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}})
instead of
{\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}}
.
In the return clause we eliminate the modality

[]\circ{\langle{\texttt{yield}}\rangle}=[{\texttt{yield}}]
of
x
.
In contrast, the omitted return clause of
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{Met}}(\mathcal{X})}
is
{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\texttt{yield}}\rangle}}\;x^{\prime}=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x^{\prime}
.

The new handler rule requires the modality
\mu
to have a comonadic
structure as specified by the conditions
\Gamma\vdash\mu\Rightarrow{\langle\rangle}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mu\Rightarrow\mu\circ\mu\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
.
These conditions are important because semantically a handler for
operation
\ell
may not be used (when
\ell
is not invoked) or be
used multiple times (when
\ell
is invoked multiple times).
Intuitively, each use of the handler consumes one modality
\mu
.
The condition
\mu\Rightarrow{\langle\rangle}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
makes sure that when the handler
is not used, we can transform away the modality
\mu
at effect
context
F
.
The condition
\mu\Rightarrow\mu\circ\mu\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
makes sure that when the
handler is used multiple times, we can duplicate the modality
\mu

each time the handlers is used.
For example, the identity modality
{\langle\rangle}
trivially satisfies the
comonadic structure, and the absolute modality
[E]
satisfies the
comonadic structure at
F
with
E\leqslant F
.

3.6.
Operational Semantics

We adopt the generative semantics of
Biernacki et al. 2020
for local
labels.
Each local label
\ell
introduced by
{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M

is replaced by a fresh label generated at runtime.
We manage these labels in a context defined as
\Omega::=\cdot\mid\Omega,\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B
.
We do not syntactically distinguish runtime generated labels from
static labels; runtime labels are tracked in
\Omega
.
We define value normal forms
U
which cannot reduce further.
The definitions for all new syntax and the operational semantics are
given in
Figure

2
.
The reduction relation has the form
M\mid\Omega\mathrel{\leadsto}N\mid\Omega
.
We omit
\Omega
when it is unchanged. Only
E-Gen
extends

\Omega
.
All judgements defined previously are also straightforwardly extended
with
\Omega
.
For instance, typing judgements are of form

\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
for runtime terms.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Value normal forms}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&U&::=&x\mid\lambda x^{A}.M\mid\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\mid{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;U\\
\textrm{Evaluation Contexts}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\mathcal{E}&::=&[~]\mid\mathcal{E}\;N\mid U\;\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}\;A\mid{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\mathcal{E}\mid\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M\\
\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}@{}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\text{{E-App}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&(\lambda x^{A}.M)\,U&\mathrel{\leadsto}&M[U/x]\\
\text{{E-TApp}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&(\Lambda\alpha^{K}.U)\,A&\mathrel{\leadsto}&U[A/\alpha]\\
\text{{E-Letmod}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,U\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M&\mathrel{\leadsto}&M[U/x]\\
\text{{E-Gen}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M\mid\Omega&\mathrel{\leadsto}&M[\ell^{\prime}/\ell]\mid\Omega,\ell^{\prime}:A\twoheadrightarrow B\hfill\text{where }\ell^{\prime}\text{ fresh}\\
\text{{E-Ret}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;U\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\mathrel{\leadsto}&N[({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{(\mu\circ{\langle\ell\rangle})}\,U)/x],\\
\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&&&\lx@intercol\hfil\text{where }H=\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N,\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N^{\prime}\}\lx@intercol\\
\text{{E-Op}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;U]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\mathrel{\leadsto}&N[U/p,({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\mu}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H))/r]\\
\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\lx@intercol\hfil\text{where }\ell\notin\mathsf{bl}(\mathcal{E})\text{ and }H\ni(\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N)\lx@intercol\\
\text{{E-Lift}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\mathcal{E}[M]&\mathrel{\leadsto}&\mathcal{E}[N]\hfill\text{if }M\mathrel{\leadsto}N\\
\end{array}

Figure 2.
Operational semantics of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).

The operational semantics mostly follows
Met
.
Rule
E-Gen
is new and generates a fresh runtime label for a
local label binding.
Moreover, since we generalise the handler of
Met
, rules
E-ret

and
E-Op
are also generalised.
Rule
E-Ret
wraps the return value with the modality

\mu\circ{\langle\ell\rangle}
.
Rule
E-Op
wraps the continuation with the modality
\mu
.
The modalities in both rules are consistent with the typing rule

T-Handle
in
Section

3.5
.
The function
\mathsf{bl}(\mathcal{E})
gives the set of bound operation labels which
have handlers installed in the evaluation context
\mathcal{E}
.
The condition
\ell\notin\mathsf{bl}(\mathcal{E})
makes sure each operation
\ell
is
handled by the dynamically innermost handler of
\ell
.

3.7.
Type Soundness and Effect Safety

To state syntactic type soundness, we first define normal forms.

Definition 3.6 (Normal Forms).

We say that term
M
is in normal form with respect to effect
context
E
, if it is either in value normal form
M=U
or of the
form
M=\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;U]
for
\ell\leqslant E
.

The following theorems together give type soundness and effect safety.
They hold for any effect theory
\mathcal{X}
satisfying the validity
conditions of
Definition

3.5
.

Theorem 3.7 (Progress).

In
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) where
\mathcal{X}
satisfies the validity conditions, if

\,\Omega\mid\cdot\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
, then either

M\mid\Omega\mathrel{\leadsto}N\mid\Omega^{\prime}
for some
N
and

\Omega^{\prime}
, or
M
is in a normal form with respect to
E
.

Theorem 3.8 (Subject Reduction).

In
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) where
\mathcal{X}
satisfies the validity conditions, if

\,\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
and
M\mid\Omega\mathrel{\leadsto}N\mid\Omega^{\prime}
, then
\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Gamma\vdash N:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
.

The proofs are given in
Appendix

C
.

4.
Encoding a Row-Based Effect System à la
Koka

In this section, we briefly present
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}

(
Xie et al. 2020
)
, a System
F-style core calculus formalising the row-based effect system of

Koka

(
Leijen 2025
)
, and show how to encode it into
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).
We refer to
Xie et al. 2020
for a complete introduction to
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
.

4.1.
System
\textsf{F}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}

The syntax of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is as follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Value Types}&A,B&::=&{\texttt{1}}\mid\alpha\mid A\to^{E}B\mid\forall\alpha^{K}.A\\
\textrm{Effect Rows}&E,F&::=&\cdot\mid\varepsilon\mid\ell,E\\
\textrm{Kind}&K&::=&\mathsf{Effect}\mid\mathsf{Value}\\
\textrm{Contexts}&\Gamma&::=&\cdot\mid\Gamma,x:A\mid\Gamma,\alpha:K\\
\textrm{Label Contexts}&\Sigma&::=&\cdot\mid\Sigma,{\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B}\\
\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Values}&V,W&::=&{()}\mid x\mid\lambda^{E}x^{A}.M\\
&&\mid&\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\mid{{{\mathbsf{handler}}}\;H}\\
\textrm{Computations}&M,N&::=&{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;V\mid V\;W\mid V\,A\\
&&\mid&{{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;V}\mid{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N\\
\textrm{Handlers}&H&::=&\{\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N\}\\
\end{array}

Different from
Xie et al. 2022
, our version of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
is fine-grain
call-by-value
(
Levy et al. 2003
)
.
Effect rows
E
are scoped rows
(
Leijen 2005
)
with an optional tail
effect variable
\varepsilon
.
As in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), we assume a fixed global label context
\Sigma
.
By convention we write
\varepsilon
for effect variables and
\alpha
for
value type variables.
We omit their kinds,
\mathsf{Effect}
and
\mathsf{Value}
, when obvious.
In type abstraction and application, we let
A
range over both value
types and effect rows, and let
\alpha
range over their type
variables.

Typing judgements in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
include
\Gamma\vdash V:A\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}
for values
and
\Gamma\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{E}{}{\mid{E}}
for computations, where the latter tracks
effects
E
.
The typing rules and operational semantics of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
are standard for a
System F-style calculus with effect handlers and a row-based effect
system
(
Hillerström and Lindley 2016
;
Leijen 2017
)
.
We provide the full rules in
Section

D.1
and
show three representative typing rules here.

\frac{\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B\@ifmtarg{E}{}{\mid{E}}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{E}x^{A}.M:A\to^{E}B\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Abs}}}\qquad\qquad\frac{\Sigma\ni\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V:A\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;V:B\@ifmtarg{\ell,E}{}{\mid{\ell,E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Do}}}\qquad\qquad\frac{H=\{\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N\}\quad\Sigma\ni\ell:A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow B^{\prime}\quad\quad\Gamma,p:A^{\prime},r:B^{\prime}\to^{E}A\vdash N:A\@ifmtarg{E}{}{\mid{E}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash\text{${{\mathbsf{handler}}}$}\;H:({\texttt{1}}\to^{\ell,E}A)\to^{E}A\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Handler}}}

Rule
T-Abs
introduces a
\lambda
-abstraction.
Rule
T-Do
invokes an operation
\ell
.
Rule
T-Handler
introduces a handler as a function that takes an
argument function of type
{\texttt{1}}\to^{\ell,E}A
as in

Section

2.5.2
.

Xie et al. 2020
do not include a return clause in handlers for

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
.

4.2.
Encoding
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
into
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
)

\hskip-9.24994pt\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Kind}\to\text{Kind}\\
\llbracket\mathsf{Effect}\rrbracket&=&\mathsf{Effect}\\
\llbracket\mathsf{Value}\rrbracket&=&\mathsf{Abs}\\[7.74997pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Effect Row}\to\text{Effect Context}\\
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket&=&\cdot\\
\llbracket\varepsilon\rrbracket&=&\varepsilon\\
\llbracket\ell,E\rrbracket&=&\ell,\llbracket E\rrbracket\\[7.74997pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Label Context}\to\text{Label Context}\\
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket&=&\cdot\\
\llbracket\Sigma,\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\rrbracket&=&\llbracket\Sigma\rrbracket,\ell:\llbracket A\rrbracket\twoheadrightarrow\llbracket B\rrbracket\\[7.74997pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Value / Handler}\to\text{Term}\\
\llbracket{()}\rrbracket&=&{()}\\
\llbracket x\rrbracket&=&x\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\rrbracket&=&\Lambda\alpha^{\llbracket K\rrbracket}.\llbracket V\rrbracket\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket\lambda^{E}x^{A}.M\rrbracket&=&{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}\;(\lambda x^{\llbracket A\rrbracket}.\llbracket M\rrbracket)\\[1.93748pt]
\Bigl\llbracket{\vbox{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{handler}}}\; H:\\
\text{\small$({\texttt{1}}\to^{\ell,E}A)\to^{E}A$}\end{array}}}\Bigr\rrbracket&=&{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}\;(\lambda f.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}\;({{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket\ell,E\rrbracket]}}\;f^{\prime}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f^{\prime}\;{()})\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\llbracket H^{E}\rrbracket)\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket\{\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N\}^{E}\rrbracket&=&\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket\ell,E\rrbracket]}}\;x^{\prime}=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x^{\prime},\ell\;p\;r\mapsto\llbracket N\rrbracket\}\end{array}\hfill\hskip-148.34747pt\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Type}\to\text{Type}\\
\llbracket{\texttt{1}}\rrbracket&=&{\texttt{1}}\\
\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket&=&\alpha\\
\llbracket A\to^{E}B\rrbracket&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}}(\llbracket A\rrbracket\to\llbracket B\rrbracket)\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket\forall\alpha^{K}.A\rrbracket&=&\forall\alpha^{\llbracket K\rrbracket}.\llbracket A\rrbracket\\[7.74997pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Context}\to\text{Context}\\
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket&=&\cdot\\
\llbracket\Gamma,x:A\rrbracket&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,x:\llbracket A\rrbracket\\
\llbracket\Gamma,\alpha:K\rrbracket&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\alpha:\llbracket K\rrbracket\\[7.74997pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Computation}\to\text{Term}\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;V\rrbracket&=&\llbracket V\rrbracket\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N\rrbracket&=&{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=\llbracket M\rrbracket\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\llbracket N\rrbracket\\
\llbracket V\;A\rrbracket&=&\llbracket V\rrbracket\;\llbracket A\rrbracket\\
\llbracket(V:A\to^{E}B)\;W\rrbracket&=&{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}\;x=\llbracket V\rrbracket\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x\;\llbracket W\rrbracket\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;V\rrbracket&=&{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;\llbracket V\rrbracket\\
\end{array}

Figure 3
.
An encoding of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
in
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).

Figure

3
encodes
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
in
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).
The translation is mostly straightforward.

For kinds, we translate effect kind
\mathsf{Effect}
to effect kind
\mathsf{Effect}

and value kind
\mathsf{Value}
to the kind
\mathsf{Abs}
.
We always translate values in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
into values of kind
\mathsf{Abs}
in

Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).

For types, we decouples effects from function types in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
by
translating an effectful function type
A\to^{E}B
into a function type
with an absolute modality
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}}(\llbracket A\rrbracket\to\llbracket B\rrbracket)
.

For contexts, we homomorphically translate each entry.

For terms, the translation is type-directed and essentially defined on
typing judgements.
We annotate components of a term with their types as necessary.
We highlight modality-relevant syntax of the term translation in

grey
.
The grey parts show how modalities decouple effect tracking.
The black parts themselves remain valid programs after type erasure.

The translation of lambda abstraction
\lambda^{E}x^{A}.M
introduces
an absolute modality by
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}
, and the translation of
function application
V\;W
first eliminates the modality of
\llbracket V\rrbracket

by
\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}\;x=\llbracket V\rrbracket
before applying it.
Examples for translations of lambda abstraction and application can be
found in
Section

2.2
as

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\rrbracket
.

Translations of type abstraction, type application, operation
invocation, and let-binding are homomorphic.
Let-binding in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) is syntactic sugar defined in the standard way
as
{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N\doteq(\lambda x.N)\;M
.
The translation of
{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;V
is simply
\llbracket V\rrbracket
.

A handler value
{{\mathbsf{handler}}}\;H
of type

({\texttt{1}}\to^{\ell,E}A)\to^{E}A
is translated to a function
that takes a function argument
f
and handles it.
We eliminate the modality of
f
before applying it to
{()}
since

f
has type
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket\ell,E\rrbracket]}}}({\texttt{1}}\to\llbracket A\rrbracket)
.
We introduce a modality
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}
for the whole translated
function since
{{\mathbsf{handler}}}\;H
is an effectful function with effect
E
.
In the return clause of
\llbracket H\rrbracket
, we need to eliminate the modality of

x
as shown in the typing rule
T-Handle
of
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).
This modality elimination is always possible because the type
\llbracket A\rrbracket

of
x
always has kind
\mathsf{Abs}
.
The operation clause of
\llbracket H\rrbracket
shows the importance of our
modality-parameterised handlers.
Note that rule
T-Handler
of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
gives the continuation
r
in

H
the type
B^{\prime}\to^{E}A
.
By annotating
{{\mathbsf{handle}}}
with
[\llbracket E\rrbracket]
, the continuation
r
in

\llbracket H\rrbracket
has type
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket E\rrbracket]}}}(\llbracket B^{\prime}\rrbracket\to\llbracket A\rrbracket)
,
which is equal to
\llbracket B^{\prime}\to^{E}A\rrbracket
.
We have partly shown the translation of the handler

\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
in
Section

2.5.2
.
We provide its full translation.

\begin{array}[]{r@{}c@{}l}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}$}}\rrbracket&\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{=}}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\Lambda\varepsilon.{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\varepsilon]}}~(\lambda f^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}})}.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{[\varepsilon]}\;({{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon]}}\;f^{\prime}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}f\;{()})\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\\
\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto{{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon]}}\;x^{\prime}=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}x^{\prime},\ell\;p\;r\mapsto p+({{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\varepsilon]}}\;r^{\prime}=r\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}r^{\prime}\;{()})\})\\
\end{array}\\
&:&\forall\varepsilon.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\varepsilon]}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{\texttt{yield}},\varepsilon]}}}({\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}})\to{\texttt{Int}})\end{array}

We have the following type and semantics preservation theorems with
proofs in
Section

E.1
.

Theorem 4.1 (Type Preservation).

If
\,\Gamma\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{E}{}{\mid{E}}
in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
, then
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\vdash\llbracket M\rrbracket:\llbracket A\rrbracket\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\llbracket E\rrbracket}}
in

Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
).
Similarly for typing judgements of values.

Theorem 4.2 (Semantics Preservation).

If
M
is well-typed and
M\mathrel{\leadsto}N
in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
, then
\llbracket M\rrbracket\mathrel{\leadsto}^{\ast}\llbracket N\rrbracket
in
Met
(
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
) where

\mathrel{\leadsto}^{\ast}
denotes the transitive closure of
\mathrel{\leadsto}
.

5.
Encoding a Capability-Based Effect System à la
Effekt

In this section we briefly present
System C

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
,
a core calculus formalising the capability-based effect system of

Effekt

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2025
)
, and show how to encode it into

Met
(
\mathcal{S}
).
We refer to
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
for a complete introduction to

System C
.

5.1.
System C

Figure

4
gives the syntax and typing rules for

System C
, which is fine-grain call-by-value
(
Levy et al. 2003
)
and
distinguishes between first-class values
V
, blocks
P
(second-class
functions), and computations
M
.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Value Types}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&A,B&::=&{\texttt{1}}\mid{T\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;C}\\
\textrm{Block Types}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&T&::=&(\@ifmtarg{\overline{A}}{{\overline{f:T}}}{\overline{A}\@ifmtarg{{\overline{f:T}}}{}{,{\overline{f:T}}}})\Rightarrow B\\
\textrm{Capability Sets}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&C&::=&{\{\overline{f}\}}\\
\textrm{Contexts}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\Gamma&::=&\cdot\mid\Gamma,x:A\mid\Gamma,{f:^{C}T}\mid\Gamma,f:^{\ast}T\\
\textrm{Values}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&V,W&::=&x\mid{()}\mid{{{\mathbsf{box}}}\;P}\\
\textrm{Handlers}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&H&::=&\{p\;r\mapsto N\}\\
\end{array}\hfill\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Blocks}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&P,Q&::=&f\mid\{(\@ifmtarg{\overline{x:A}}{\overline{f:T}}{\overline{x:A}\@ifmtarg{\overline{f:T}}{}{,\overline{f:T}}})\Rightarrow M\}\\
\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&&\mid&{{{\mathbsf{unbox}}}\;V}\\
\textrm{Computations}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&M,N&::=&{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;V\mid P(\overline{V},\overline{Q})\\
\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N\\
\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{def}}}\;f=P\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N\\
\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{try}}}\;\{f^{(A)\Rightarrow B}\Rightarrow M\}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\\
\end{array}

[formula]
[formula]

\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash{()}:{\texttt{1}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Unit}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\ni x:A}{\Gamma\vdash x:A\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Var}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash P:T\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{box}}}\;P:T\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;C\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Box}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\ni f:^{C}T}{\Gamma\vdash f:T\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Transparent}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\ni f:^{\ast}T}{\Gamma\vdash f:T\@ifmtarg{\{f\}}{}{\mid{\{f\}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Tracked}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:T\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;C\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{unbox}}}\;V:T\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Unbox}}}\par\frac{\Gamma,{\overline{x:A},\overline{f:^{\ast}T}}\vdash M:B\@ifmtarg{C\cup\{\overline{f}\}}{}{\mid{C\cup\{\overline{f}\}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\{(\@ifmtarg{\overline{x:A}}{\overline{f:T}}{\overline{x:A}\@ifmtarg{\overline{f:T}}{}{,\overline{f:T}}})\Rightarrow M\}:(\@ifmtarg{\overline{A}}{\overline{f:T}}{\overline{A}\@ifmtarg{\overline{f:T}}{}{,\overline{f:T}}})\Rightarrow B\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Block}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash P:T\@ifmtarg{C^{\prime}}{}{\mid{C^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994ptC^{\prime}\subseteq C}{\Gamma\vdash P:T\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-BSub}}}

[formula]

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:A\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;V:A\@ifmtarg{\cdot}{}{\mid{\cdot}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Value}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:A\vdash N:B\@ifmtarg{C^{\prime}}{}{\mid{C^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N:B\@ifmtarg{C\cup C^{\prime}}{}{\mid{C\cup C^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Let}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash P:(\@ifmtarg{\overline{A_{i}}}{\overline{f_{j}:T_{j}}}{\overline{A_{i}}\@ifmtarg{\overline{f_{j}:T_{j}}}{}{,\overline{f_{j}:T_{j}}}})\Rightarrow B\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{\Gamma\vdash V_{i}:A_{i}\@ifmtarg{}{}{\mid{}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{\Gamma\vdash Q_{j}:T_{j}\@ifmtarg{C_{j}}{}{\mid{C_{j}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash P(\overline{V_{i}},\overline{Q_{j}}):B[\overline{C_{j}/f_{j}}]\@ifmtarg{C\cup\overline{C_{j}}}{}{\mid{C\cup\overline{C_{j}}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Call}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{C^{\prime}}{}{\mid{C^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptC^{\prime}\subseteq C}{\Gamma\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Sub}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash P:T\@ifmtarg{C^{\prime}}{}{\mid{C^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,f:^{C^{\prime}}T\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{def}}}\;f=P\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M:A\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Def}}}\par\frac{\Gamma,{f:^{\ast}(\@ifmtarg{A^{\prime}}{}{A^{\prime}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow B^{\prime}}\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{C\cup\{f\}}{}{\mid{C\cup\{f\}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,p:A^{\prime},r:^{C}(\@ifmtarg{B^{\prime}}{}{B^{\prime}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow A\vdash N:A\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{try}}}\;\{f^{(\@ifmtarg{A^{\prime}}{}{A^{\prime}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow B^{\prime}}\Rightarrow M\}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{p\;r\mapsto N\}:A\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Handle}}}

Figure 4.
Syntax and typing rules for
System C
. We mostly follow the
syntax of
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
. The main difference is that we
write
\Rightarrow
for block types to emphasise they are second-class.

We have three typing judgements for values, blocks, and computations
individually.
Judgements for blocks
\Gamma\vdash P:T\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}
and computations

\Gamma\vdash M:A\@ifmtarg{C}{}{\mid{C}}
explicitly track a capability set
C
, which
contains the capabilities in
\Gamma
that may be used.

The typing rules of
System C
are much more involved than those of

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
as capability tracking is deeply entangled with term constructs
such as block constructions (
T-Block
), block calls
(
T-Call
), block bindings (
T-Def
), and usages of block
variables (
T-Transparent
and
T-Tracked
).
Due to space constraints, we focus on explaining these key rules.

There are two rules for uses of block variables as there are two forms
of block variable bindings in contexts.
A
tracked
binding
f:^{\ast}T
stands for a capability.
Rule
T-Tracked
tracks
f
itself in the singleton capability set

\{f\}
.
A
transparent
binding
f:^{C}T
stands for a user-defined block
whose capability set
C
is known.
Rule
T-Transparent
tracks
C
as the capability set.

Rules
T-Def
and
T-Block
both bind block variables.
Rule
T-Def
binds a block
P
as a transparent block
variable
f:^{C^{\prime}}T
where
C^{\prime}
is the capability set of
P
.
Rule
T-Block
binds a list of tracked block variables
(capabilities)
\overline{f:^{\ast}T}
whose concrete capability sets are
unknown until called.
The rule
T-Block
reflects the roles that block constructions
play for capability tracking as we introduced in

Section

2.3.3
.
For instance, all capabilities
\overline{f}
are added to the capability
set of the block body
M
.

Rule
T-Call
fully applies a block
P
to values
\overline{V_{i}}
and
blocks
\overline{Q_{j}}
.
The rule reflects the roles that block calls play for capability
tracking as we introduced in
Section

2.3.4
.
It substitutes each block variable
{f_{j}}
(recall that these
variables are bound as
f_{j}:^{\ast}T
in rule
T-Block
) with
the capability set
C_{j}
of the block
Q_{j}
in type
B
.
The capability set of the call is the union of the capability sets of

P
and all its block arguments because all these arguments might be
invoked.

Rule
T-Handle
defines a named handler which introduces a
capability
f:(\@ifmtarg{A^{\prime}}{}{A^{\prime}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow B^{\prime}
to the scope of
M
.
Operation invocation via calling
f
in
M
is handled by this
handler.
The capability
f
is added to the capability set of
M
.
The continuation
r
is introduced as a transparent binding with
capability set
C
as it may only use capabilities in
C
provided by
the context.

System C
adopts named handlers and a generative semantics with a
reduction relation
M\mid\Omega\mathrel{\leadsto}N\mid\Omega^{\prime}
where

\Omega::=\cdot\mid\ell:(\@ifmtarg{A}{}{A\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow B
is a context for
runtime operation labels, similar to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).
The most interesting reduction rule is
E-Gen
which uses a
runtime capability value
{{\mathbsf{cap}}}_{\ell}
with a runtime label
\ell
to
substitute a capability
f
introduced by a handler.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}@{}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\text{{E-Gen}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{{\mathbsf{try}}}\;\{f^{(\@ifmtarg{A}{}{A\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow B}\Rightarrow M\}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\mid\Omega&\mathrel{\leadsto}&{{\mathbsf{try}}}_{\ell}\;M[{{\mathbsf{cap}}}_{\ell}/f]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\mid\Omega,\ell:(\@ifmtarg{A}{}{A\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow B\hskip 9.24994pt\text{where }\ell\text{ fresh}\end{array}

The full specification of operational semantics can be found in

Section

D.2
.

5.2.
Encoding
System C
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
)

Figure

5
encodes
System C
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
).
The term translation is type-directed and defined on typing
judgements.
We annotate components of a term with their types and capability sets
as necessary.
We highlight syntax relevant to modalities and type abstraction of the
term translation in
grey
.
The grey parts show how modalities decouple capability tracking.
The black parts remain valid programs after type erasure.
The encoding is unavoidably more involved than that of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
because
of the deeper entanglement of capability tracking with blocks.
As in
Section

5.1
, we focus on explaining the encoding of
block-relevant constructs.

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Cap Set}\to\text{Effect Context}\\
\llbracket\{\overline{f}\}\rrbracket&=&\overline{{f}^{\ast}}\\[7.74997pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Value / Block Type}\to\text{Type}\\
\llbracket{\texttt{1}}\rrbracket&=&{\texttt{1}}\\
\llbracket T\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;C\rrbracket&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket(\@ifmtarg{\overline{A}}{\overline{f:T}}{\overline{A}\@ifmtarg{\overline{f:T}}{}{,\overline{f:T}}})\Rightarrow B\rrbracket&=&\forall\overline{{f}^{\ast}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\overline{{f}^{\ast}}\rangle}}}}(\overline{\llbracket A\rrbracket}\to\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket)\\[7.74997pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Value}\to\text{Term}\\
\llbracket{()}\rrbracket&=&{()}\\
\llbracket x\rrbracket&=&x\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{box}}}\;P:T\;{{\mathbsf{at}}}\;C\rrbracket&=&{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}\;\llbracket P\rrbracket\\[7.74997pt]
\end{array}\hfill\hskip-29.59976pt\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Context}\to\text{Context}\\
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket&=&\cdot\\
\llbracket\Gamma,x:A\rrbracket&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,x:\llbracket A\rrbracket\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket\Gamma,f:^{\ast}T\rrbracket&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,{f}^{\ast},{f}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}{\llbracket T\rrbracket},\hat{f}\@ifmtarg{[{f}^{\ast}]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket\Gamma,f:^{C}T\rrbracket&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,{f}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}}{\llbracket T\rrbracket},\hat{f}\@ifmtarg{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket\\[3.87498pt]
\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Block}\to\text{Term}\\
\llbracket f\rrbracket&=&\hat{f}\\
\llbracket\{({\overline{x:A}},{\overline{f:T}})\Rightarrow{M}\}\rrbracket&=&{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}\Lambda\overline{{f}^{\ast}}.{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\overline{{f}^{\ast}}\rangle}}}\;(\lambda\overline{x^{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}\,\overline{f^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[{f}^{\ast}]}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket}}.\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\overline{{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{f}^{\ast}]}}\;\hat{f}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}\llbracket M\rrbracket)\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{unbox}}}\;V:T\mid C\rrbracket&=&{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}\;x=\,\llbracket V\rrbracket\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x\\[7.74997pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket-\rrbracket&:&\text{Computation / Handler}\to\text{Term}\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;V\rrbracket&=&\llbracket V\rrbracket\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N\rrbracket&=&{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;x=\llbracket M\rrbracket\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\llbracket N\rrbracket\\
\llbracket{{\mathbsf{def}}}\;f=P:T\mid C\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N\rrbracket&=&{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;f={\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}\,\llbracket P\rrbracket\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}\;}\hat{f}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\llbracket N\rrbracket\\[3.87498pt]
\llbracket P(\overline{V_{i}},\overline{Q_{j}:T_{j}\mid C_{j}})\rrbracket&=&{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\overline{\llbracket C_{j}\rrbracket}\rangle}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x=\llbracket P\rrbracket}}\;\overline{\llbracket C_{j}\rrbracket}}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x\;\overline{\llbracket V_{i}\rrbracket}\;\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket C_{j}\rrbracket]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\llbracket Q_{j}\rrbracket}})}}\\[1.93748pt]
\Bigl\llbracket\vbox{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{try}}}\; \lx@text@lbrace f^{
(
\@ifmtarg{A'}{}{A'
\@ifmtarg{}{}{, }
}
)
\Rightarrow B'
}\Rightarrow M\lx@text@rbrace\\
\!\!\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H : A\mid C\end{array}}\Bigr\rrbracket&=&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell_{f}:\llbracket A^{\prime}\rrbracket\twoheadrightarrow\llbracket B^{\prime}\rrbracket\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\ell_{f}\rangle}}}\;g=\\[1.93748pt]
\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}({\Lambda{f}^{\ast}}.{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{f}^{\ast}\rangle}}\;(}\lambda f.{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{f}^{\ast}]}}\;\hat{f}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\llbracket M\rrbracket{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}))\;\ell_{f}}\\[3.87498pt]
\!\!\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}\;(g\;({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{f}]}}\;({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}}\;(\lambda x^{\llbracket A^{\prime}\rrbracket}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{f}\;x))))\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\llbracket H^{f,C}\rrbracket\end{array}\\[1.93748pt]
\llbracket\{p\;r\mapsto N\}^{f,C}\rrbracket&=&\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{f},\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}\;x^{\prime}=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x^{\prime},\\
\ell_{f}\;p\;r\mapsto{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}\;\hat{r}=r\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\llbracket N\rrbracket\}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 5
.
An encoding of
System C
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
).

For block constructions and block calls, we have explained the
encodings of them in details in

Sections

2.3.3
and
2.3.4
,
using the constructions and calls of blocks
\text{$\mathit{app}$}_{C}
and

\text{$\mathit{app^{\prime}}$}_{C}
as examples.

A block binding
{{\mathbsf{def}}}\;f=P\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N
not only binds a block
P
to
f

but also annotate the binding
f:^{C^{\prime}}T
with the capability set
C^{\prime}

of the block
P
as shown by rule
T-Def
in

Figure

4
.
For instance, we can bind the block
\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}
in

Section

2.3.1
to
f
and apply it to
42
.
Its typing derivation is as follows.

\frac{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:^{\ast}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mid}}\;\;\{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}\}\qquad\qquad\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:^{\ast}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}},{f:^{\{{\mathit{y}}\}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;f(42)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mid}}\;\;\{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}\}\quad}{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}:^{\ast}\@ifmtarg{}{}{(}\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{Int}}}{}{{\texttt{Int}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}}\@ifmtarg{}{}{)}\Rightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vdash}}\;\;\text{${{\mathbsf{def}}}\;$}f={\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}$}}\text{$\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;$}f(42)\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\colon}}\;{\texttt{1}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mid}}\;\;\{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}\}}

The binding of
f
in the second premise is annotated with its
capability set
\{{\mathit{y}}\}
since
\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}
uses the capability

{\mathit{y}}
.
We cannot simply encode such a transparent binding by ignoring its
annotation of the capability set.
Instead, we use an absolute modality to simulate this annotation.
To encode the binding of
f
, we wrap the translated block

\text{$\mathit{gen}$}_{C}
into the absolute modality
[{\mathit{y}}]
.
The full translation of the above term is as follows, where we provide
the omitted identity modality in

Section

2.3.1
.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{${{\mathbsf{let}}}\;f={\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}$}\;(\text{${\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}}$}\;(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.\hat{\mathit{{\mathit{y}}}}\;x))\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}\;\hat{f}=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}}\;f^{\prime}=\hat{f}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;f^{\prime}\;42\end{array}

We eliminate the modality
[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]
of
f
and bind it to

\hat{f}
, reminiscent of how we translate block arguments bound by
block constructions.
In general, for a transparent block variable binding
f:^{C}T
in
the context, it is translated to two variable bindings

f:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket
and
\hat{f}\@ifmtarg{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}}}\llbracket T\rrbracket
.

The translation of uses of block variables is simple. We translate
each
f
to its hat version
\hat{f}
.
The simplicity benefits from the fact that we eagerly eliminate the
modality of each
f
after it is introduced, e.g., in the translations
of block constructions and block bindings.

The translation of named handlers
{{\mathbsf{try}}}\;\{f^{(\@ifmtarg{A^{\prime}}{}{A^{\prime}\@ifmtarg{}{}{,}})\Rightarrow B^{\prime}}\Rightarrow M\}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H
is different from the translation of
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}
in

Section

2.5.3
.
The full translation of
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}
is as follows, where we provide
the omitted identity modality of the function
\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{{\mathit{y}}}\;x
.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{local}}}\;\ell_{\mathit{y}}:{\texttt{Int}}\twoheadrightarrow{\texttt{1}}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\ell_{\mathit{y}}\rangle}}}\;g=({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}\Lambda{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}.}{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}\rangle}}}\;(\lambda{\mathit{y}}.{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}]}}\;\hat{{\mathit{y}}}={\mathit{y}}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\hat{y}\;42;\hat{y}\;37;0))\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}\ell_{\mathit{y}}}\\
\!\!\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}\;(g\;({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{\mathit{y}}]}}\;({\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}}\;(\lambda x^{\texttt{Int}}.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{\mathit{y}}\;x))))\\
\!\!\text{$\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;$}\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto{{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{\mathit{y}},\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}\;x^{\prime}=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}x^{\prime},\ell_{\mathit{y}}\;p\;r\mapsto{{{\mathbsf{let}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.543,0.5547,0.5625}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\llbracket C\rrbracket]}}\;\hat{r}=r\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}p+\hat{r}\;{()}\}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

The main difference is that, instead of directly using the local label

\ell_{\mathit{y}}
for the handled computation, we introduce an effect
variable
{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}
first and substitute it with
\ell_{\mathit{y}}
.
This extra layer of abstraction is necessary to keep the translation
systematic, because our translations of types and terms consistently
translate a capability
{\mathit{y}}
to an effect variable
{{\mathit{y}}}^{\ast}
.
After reducing the type application and substitution of
g
in the
above translation term, we get the translation of
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}
in

Section

2.5.3
.

In the return clause, we additionally eliminate the modality of
x
.
In the operation clause, we eliminate the modality
[\llbracket C\rrbracket]
of

r
and bind it to
\hat{r}
as we use a modality-parameterised
handler.
Using a modality-parameterised handler is important because in

\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{C}
, the continuation
r
is a transparent binding of form

f:^{C}{\texttt{1}}\to{\texttt{Int}}
as shown by the typing rule
T-Handle
of

System C
in
Section

5.1
.
We need to wrap the translated continuation
r
with the absolute
modality
[\llbracket C\rrbracket]
to be consistent with the translation of
transparent bindings.

For contexts, we translate each entry.
For a variable binding
x:A
, we translate it homomorphically.
For a transparent binding of a block variable
f:^{C}T
, we translate
it to two term variables
f
and
\hat{f}
as discussed in the
translation of
{{\mathbsf{def}}}\;\!\!
above.
For a tracked binding of a block variable
f:^{\ast}T
, we translate it
to an effect variable
{f}^{\ast}
and two term variables
f
and

\hat{f}
as discussed in
Section

2.2
.

We have the following type and semantics preservation theorems with
proofs in
Section

E.2
.

Theorem 5.1 (Type Preservation).

If
\,\Gamma\vdash M:A\mid C
in
System C
, then

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\vdash\llbracket M\rrbracket:\llbracket A\rrbracket\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\llbracket C\rrbracket}}
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
).
Similarly for typing judgements of values and blocks.

Theorem 5.2 (Semantics Preservation).

If
M
is well-typed and
M\mid\Omega\mathrel{\leadsto}N\mid{\Omega^{\prime}}

in
System C
, then
\llbracket M\rrbracket\mid{\llbracket\Omega\rrbracket}\mathrel{\leadsto}^{\ast}\llbracket N\rrbracket\mid{\llbracket\Omega^{\prime}\rrbracket}
in

Met
(
\mathcal{S}
), where
\mathrel{\leadsto}^{\ast}
denotes the transitive closure
of
\mathrel{\leadsto}
.

6.
More Encodings and Discussions

In this section, we discuss more encodings of effect systems into

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
), highlight language design insights gleaned from our encodings,
and outline potential extensions to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).

6.1.
An Early Version of
Effekt

System
\Xi

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2020
)
is an early core calculus of the

Effekt
language.

System
\Xi
is essentially a fragment of
System C
without boxes.
As a result, in
System
\Xi
capabilities can never appear in types since
we cannot box a second-class block into a first-class value.
While our encoding of
System C
in
Section

5.2
directly
gives an encoding of
System
\Xi
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
), it introduces
unnecessary complexity.
Since capabilities never appear in types in
System
\Xi
, we do not need
to introduce an effect variable
{f}^{\ast}
for each capability
f
in
the encoding.
It turns out that we can simply encode second-class blocks in

System
\Xi
as first-class functions in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) without introducing
any extra term constructs.
For instance, a block
{\{(\@ifmtarg{x:A}{f:T}{x:A\@ifmtarg{f:T}{}{,f:T}})\Rightarrow M\}}
is encoded as a function

\lambda x^{\llbracket A\rrbracket}\,f^{\llbracket T\rrbracket}.\llbracket M\rrbracket
by merely changing the notations.
We provide the full encoding of
System
\Xi
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) in

Section

D.3
and prove it preserves types and semantics in

Section

E.3
.

6.2.
Named Handlers in
Koka

Xie et al. 2022
extend
Koka
with named handlers and formalise this
extension in the core calculus
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
, which is based on
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
.

System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
allows each handler to bind a handler name that can be used to
invoke operations.
A handler name is similar to a capability in
System C
but it is
a first-class value.
For instance, we can define a named handler in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
as follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}$}}}$}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{\doteq}}\;\;\Lambda\varepsilon.\text{${{\mathbsf{nhandler}}}$}\;\{{\texttt{yield}}\;p\;r\mapsto p+r\;{()}\}\;:\forall\varepsilon.(\forall a.\mathsf{ev}\,\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{yield}}}{a}{{{\texttt{yield}}}^{a}}\to^{{\texttt{yield}}^{a},\varepsilon}{\texttt{Int}})\to^{\varepsilon}{\texttt{Int}}\end{array}

This handler is similar to the handler
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
in

Section

2.5.2
.
The main difference is that the argument takes a value of type

\mathsf{ev}\,\@ifmtarg{{\texttt{yield}}}{a}{{{\texttt{yield}}}^{a}}
.
This is a first-class handler name with which we can invoke the

yield
operation.
For example, we can apply
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{System~$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}$}}}
as follows.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}\text{$\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}$}}}$}}~E~(\Lambda a.\lambda h^{\text{$\mathsf{ev}\,\@ifmtarg{\scalebox{0.9}{{yield}}}{a}{{\scalebox{0.9}{{yield}}}^{a}}$}}.h\;42;h\;37;0)\end{array}

Instead of using the label
yield
to invoke the operation as
in application of
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{$\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}$}}}
in

Section

2.5.2
, we directly apply the handler name

h
to arguments.
This is reminiscent of the handler
\text{$\mathit{sum}$}_{\textsf{{System~C}}}
in

Section

2.5.3
where we invoke the operation by
calling the capability introduced by the handler.
This program reduces to
79
.
The scope variable
a
ensure scope safety of the handler name,
similar to the technique used by
runST
in

Haskell

(
Launchbury and Jones 1995
)
.

As with the encoding of named handlers in
System C
, we can encode a
named handler of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
by introducing a local label
\ell_{a}
and
using the term
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[\ell_{a}]}\;(\lambda x.{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{a}\;x)
to
simulate the handler name.
We use the effect theory
\mathcal{S}
instead of
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
because there can
never be duplicated handlers with the same name in
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
.
The theory
\mathcal{S}
gives us flexibility to have multiple effect
variables, which we use to encode scope variables.
We provide the full encoding of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
in
Met
(
\mathcal{S}
) in

Section

D.4
and prove its type and semantics preservation in

Section

E.4
.

6.3.
Insights for Language Design

In
Section

2.4
and

Section

2.5.4
, we demonstrated how our
encodings provide a direct way to compare the differences of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and

System C
.
Moreover, our encodings can also help to inform language design
choices based on the following observations.

(1)

Our encodings together demonstrate that modal effect types
are as expressive as row-based and capability-based effect
systems we consider.

(2)

The encoding of
System
\Xi
(
Section

6.1
) implies that we
do not need to sacrifice first-class functions to obtain the
benefits of the contextual effect polymorphism of
Effekt
.

(3)

The encodings of
System C
(
Section

5.2
),
System
\Xi
(
Section

6.1
), and
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
(
Section

6.2
) demonstrate
that we can use local labels, a minimal extension as introduced in

Section

3
, to simulate the relatively heavyweight
feature of named handlers in
Effekt
and
Koka
.

(4)

The encoding of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon+\mathsf{sn}}
(
Section

3.5
) further
demonstrates that first-class handler names of
Koka
do not provide
extra expressiveness compared to second-class local labels of

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).

(5)

The encoding of
System C
(
Section

5.2
) shows that
instead of having a built-in form of capabilities which can appear
at both term and type levels as in
Effekt
and

Scala

(
Boruch-Gruszecki et al. 2023
)
, we can simulate it by
introducing an effect variable for each argument and wrap the
argument into an absolute modality with the corresponding effect
variable.

6.4.
Potential Extensions to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
)

We discuss three potential extensions to
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) and leave their
full development as future work.

Effect Kinds

We can extend the effect theory to abstract over effect kinds instead
of having a single kind
\mathsf{Effect}
. The augmented definition of effect theory is a triple
\mathcal{X}=\langle\mathbb{R},:,\equiv\rangle
where the new component
\mathbb{R}
is a set
of effect kinds.
We must extend the kinding and equivalence relations accordingly.
As an example of this extension, in order to characterise Rèmy-style
row types
(
Rémy 1989
)
which use a kind system to ensure that there
is no duplicated label, we can declare
\mathbb{R}=\{\mathsf{Row}_{\mathcal{L}}\mid\mathcal{L}\}
where
\mathcal{L}
is a label set and denotes all labels that must not
be in the row.
As another example, this extension enables us to combine different
effect theories together by assigning a kind to each theory.
For instance, we can declare two kinds
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathsf{Row}

for theories
\mathcal{S}
and
{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
respectively, and then give local
labels the kind
\mathsf{Set}
and global labels the kind
\mathsf{Row}
.
We can then treat local labels as sets and global labels as scoped
rows.

Presence Types

We can associate operation labels in extensions and effect contexts
with presence types
(
Rémy 1994
)
.
Furthermore, instead of predefining the operation types for labels, we
can assign operation types to labels in extensions and effect contexts
in the manner of
Tang et al. 2025
.
For instance, the syntax of extensions could be extended to

D::=\cdot\mid\ell:P,D\mid\varepsilon,D
, where
P
is a presence type
typically defined as
P::=-\mid\mathsf{Pre}(A\twoheadrightarrow B)\mid\theta
.
A label can be absent (
-
), present with a type (
\mathsf{Pre}(A\twoheadrightarrow B)
),
or polymorphic over its presence (
\theta
).

Masking

Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) does not include the mask operator and the mask modality

{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}
of
Met

(
Tang et al. 2025
)
.
This enables us to substantially simplify the presentation of the core
calculus, especially the definitions relevant to modalities in

Section

3.3
, compared to that of
Tang et al. 2025
.
Moreover, the lack of the mask operator does not influence our
encodings as the core calculi of
Effekt
and
Koka
do not have it.
Masking
(
Biernacki et al. 2018
;
Convent et al. 2020
)
is useful for effect
systems based on scoped rows where duplicated labels indicate nested
handlers for the same operation label.
With the mask operator, we can manually select which handler to use
when nested.
It is interesting future work to extend
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) with a suitable
notion of abstract mask operator and extend the syntax of relative
modalities to
{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}
where
L
is a mask and
D
is an
extension.
This extension will require extending the effect theory to define the
kinding and equivalence relations of masks.
A form of masking also makes sense for effect theories other than

{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{scp}}}
.
For instance, masking
\ell
from a computation in
\mathcal{S}
could be
used to disallow
\ell
to be performed by the computation.

7.
Related and Future Work

Row-Based Effect Systems

Row-based effect systems track effects by annotating function arrows
with effect types implemented by row types.
They have been adopted in research languages
Links
,
Koka
, and

Frank
.

Links

(
Hillerström and Lindley 2016
)
uses Rémy-style row types with presence
polymorphism
(
Rémy 1994
)
, while
Koka

(
Leijen 2017
)
and

Frank

(
Lindley et al. 2017
)
use scoped rows
(
Leijen 2005
)
.

Eff

(
Bauer and Pretnar 2013
)
and
Helium

(
Biernacki et al. 2020
)
also track
effects on function arrows but treat effect types as sets.
In this paper we have focused on
Koka
, but we expect that other
row-based effect systems can be encoded similarly by instantiating the
effect theory appropriately.

Capability-Based Effect Systems

Capability-based effect systems introduce and track effects as
capabilities.
Different variations diverge on when capability sets appear in types.

Effekt

(
Brachthäuser et al. 2020
;
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
uses second-class
functions and only attaches capability sets to types when boxing
functions.

\textsf{{CC}}_{<:\Box}

(
Boruch-Gruszecki et al. 2023
)
, the basis for capture
tracking in
Scala
3, always annotates every type with its capability set and uses subtyping
to simplify capability sets.
It is interesting future work to encode
\textsf{{CC}}_{<:\Box}
in
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).

Abstracting Effect Systems

Yoshioka et al. 2024
study different treatments of effect collections
in row-based effect systems.
They propose a parameterised core calculus,
\lambda_{\mathrm{EA}}
, whose effect
types can be instantiated to various kinds of sets and rows.
The effect types in
\lambda_{\mathrm{EA}}
are still entangled with function types.
As a result,
\lambda_{\mathrm{EA}}
cannot encode capability-based effect systems.
We follow
\lambda_{\mathrm{EA}}
in parameterising our core calculus
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
)
over different treatments of effect collections.
We make use of modalities to decouple effect tracking from function
types, enabling the encodings of both row-based and capability-based
effect systems.

Encoding into Modal Effect Types

Tang et al. 2025
encode a fragment of row-based effect systems
where each effect type can only refer to the lexically closest effect
variable into
Met
without using effect polymorphism.
They use this encoding to demonstrate that
Met
reduces the
requirement of effect variables.
However, they lack a comparison between the full expressive power of

Met
and other effect systems.
Our encodings fill this gap and show that modal effect types
are as expressive as many row-based and capability-based effect
systems in the literature.

Effectful Contextual Modal Type Theory

Zyuzin and Nanevski 2021
propose effectful contextual modal type theory
(ECMTT) which extends the
contextual necessity
modality

(
Nanevski et al. 2008
)

to track contexts of effectful operations.
The contextual necessity modality is similar to the absolute modality
in
Met

(
Tang et al. 2025
)
.
However, ECMTT does not achieve the same level of decoupling between
effect tracking and function types as
Met
does.
In particular, ECMTT requires functions to be pure; in order to define
an effectful function we must box the function body with a modality.
This restriction forces every effectful function type in ECMTT to be
accompanied by a modality which specifies the effects used by the
function.
In contrast,
Met
provides more flexibility over when modalities
appear in types and are used to track effects.
This flexibility is especially important for the encoding of
System C
in
Section

5.2
.
We opt for
Met
as the basis of our framework
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
).

Call-By-Push-Value

Attempts to decouple programming language features have frequently
born fruit.
For instance, call-by-push-value (CBPV)
(
Levy 2004
)
subsumes both
call-by-value (CBV) and call-by-name (CBN) by decoupling thunking and
forcing from function abstraction and application.
Our work is in a similar vein.
More interestingly, our encodings of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
and
System C
possess
certain similarities with Levy’s encodings of CBV and CBN into CBPV,
respectively.
In our encoding of
System
\textsf{F}^{\epsilon}
, each function is wrapped in an absolute
modality, reminiscent of the CBV-to-CBPV encoding where each function
is thunked.
In our encoding of
System C
, we only wrap a block in an absolute
modality when it is passed, reminiscent of the CBN-to-CBPV encoding
where thunking is deferred until being passed.
We are interested in further exploring these similarities.

Expressive Power of Effect Handlers

Forster et al. 2019
compare the expressive power of effect handlers,
monadic reflection, and delimited control in a simply-typed setting
and show that delimited control cannot encode effect handlers in a
type-preserving way.

Piróg et al. 2019
extend the comparison between effect handlers and
delimited control to a polymorphic setting and show their equivalence.

Ikemori et al. 2023
further show the typed equivalence between named
handlers and multi-prompt delimited control.
In contrast to these works, which compare effect handlers with
other programming abstractions, we compare different effect systems
for effect handlers.

Future Work

In addition to the ideas already discussed above and in

Section

6.4
, other directions for future work
include: exploring inverse encodings (from instantiations of
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) into other calculi); studying parametricity and abstraction
safety
(
Biernacki et al. 2020
;
Zhang and Myers 2019
)
for
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
); and further
developing
Met
(
\mathcal{X}
) as a uniform intermediate language for type- and
effect-directed optimisation.

\mathcal{S}
,

{\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{simp}}}
, and

.

\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\cdot:\mathsf{Effect}}\par\frac{\Sigma,\Gamma\ni\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\quad\Gamma\vdash D:\mathsf{Effect}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash\ell,D:\mathsf{Effect}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash\varepsilon:\mathsf{Effect}\quad\Gamma\vdash D:\mathsf{Effect}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash\varepsilon,D:\mathsf{Effect}}

[formula]

\frac{}{D\equiv D}\par\frac{D_{1}\equiv D_{2}\quad D_{2}\equiv D_{3}}{D_{1}\equiv D_{3}}\par\frac{D\equiv D^{\prime}}{\ell,D\equiv\ell,D^{\prime}}\par\frac{D\equiv D^{\prime}}{\varepsilon,D\equiv\varepsilon,D^{\prime}}\par\frac{}{\ell,\ell^{\prime},D\equiv\ell^{\prime},\ell,D}\par\frac{}{\ell,\varepsilon,D\equiv\varepsilon,\ell,D}\par\frac{}{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime},D\equiv\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon,D}\par\frac{}{\ell,\ell,D\equiv\ell,D}\par\frac{}{\varepsilon,\varepsilon,D\equiv\varepsilon,D}

\Sigma

\cdot\vdash A\twoheadrightarrow B
to hold for every

.

\par\frac{\Gamma\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma,\alpha:K\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash A\twoheadrightarrow B\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma,\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}
